### EvolveSignal: A Large Language Model Powered Coding Agent for Discovering Traffic Signal Control Algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03335v2)
- **Authors**: Leizhen Wang, Peibo Duan, Hao Wang, Yue Wang, Jian Xu, Nan Zheng, Zhenliang Ma
- **Abstract**: In traffic engineering, the fixed-time traffic signal control remains widely used for its low cost, stability, and interpretability. However, its design depends on hand-crafted formulas (e.g., Webster) and manual re-timing by engineers to adapt to demand changes, which is labor-intensive and often yields suboptimal results under heterogeneous or congested conditions. This paper introduces the EvolveSignal, a large language models (LLMs) powered coding agent to automatically discover new traffic signal control algorithms. We formulate the problem as program synthesis, where candidate algorithms are represented as Python functions with fixed input-output structures, and iteratively optimized through external evaluations (e.g., a traffic simulator) and evolutionary search. Experiments on a signalized intersection demonstrate that the discovered algorithms outperform Webster's baseline, reducing average delay by 20.1% and average stops by 47.1%. Beyond performance, ablation and incremental analyses reveal that EvolveSignal modifications-such as adjusting cycle length bounds, incorporating right-turn demand, and rescaling green allocations-can offer practically meaningful insights for traffic engineers. This work opens a new research direction by leveraging AI for algorithm design in traffic signal control, bridging program synthesis with transportation engineering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the EvolveSignal paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EvolveSignal, an LLM-powered coding agent designed to automatically discover and optimize fixed-time traffic signal control algorithms.  It formulates the problem as program synthesis, where candidate algorithms are Python functions iteratively improved through simulation-based evaluation and evolutionary search.  Experiments on a signalized intersection demonstrate that the discovered algorithms outperform Webster's method, reducing average delay and stops.  The paper also analyzes specific modifications made by EvolveSignal, providing insights into practically meaningful adjustments for traffic engineers, such as cycle length bounds, right-turn demand inclusion, and green allocation rescaling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Novel Approach:** The paper's primary strength lies in its novel application of LLMs and program synthesis to a problem traditionally solved with empirical heuristics.  Bridging the gap between AI-driven algorithm discovery and the practical domain of traffic signal control is a promising direction.
    * **Practical Relevance:**  The focus on fixed-time control is significant. While adaptive control receives more attention, fixed-time control remains widely deployed due to its simplicity and cost-effectiveness. Improving fixed-time strategies has direct real-world impact.
    * **Explainable AI:**  The paper emphasizes the interpretability of the discovered algorithms. LLMs provide natural language justifications for their code modifications, allowing traffic engineers to understand and adapt the synthesized strategies. This is a significant advantage over black-box optimization methods.
    * **Performance Gains:**  The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements over a well-established baseline (Webster's method) in a simulated environment.
    * **Detailed Analysis:**  The ablation and incremental analyses are valuable. They provide insights into the individual and combined effects of the modifications, revealing synergistic relationships and non-intuitive design patterns.
    * **Open Source and Reproducibility:** The referencing of open-source projects (AlphaEvolve, OpenEvolve, SUMO) suggests an intent to promote reproducibility, which is crucial for scientific credibility.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Scope:** The experiments are confined to a single, isolated four-leg intersection and three specific demand scenarios. While this allows for controlled evaluation, it raises concerns about the generalizability of the results to more complex intersections, varying traffic conditions, and network-wide optimization.
    * **Simulation Fidelity:**  The reliance on SUMO simulations, while a common practice, introduces potential discrepancies between the simulated environment and real-world traffic dynamics. The performance gains may not fully translate to real-world deployments.
    * **Simplified Objective Function:** The objective function primarily considers delay and stops.  Other important factors, such as safety, emissions, and equity, are not explicitly addressed. This simplification could lead to suboptimal solutions when considering broader societal impacts.
    * **LLM Dependence and Scalability:** The framework heavily relies on LLMs, which have inherent limitations.  LLM performance can vary significantly depending on the prompt engineering, the specific model used, and the available computational resources.  Scaling EvolveSignal to larger and more complex traffic networks might pose challenges.
    * **Lack of comparison with other algorithms:** The paper only compares the result with the Webster's baseline method, without comparing with other algorithms that are capable of providing results to compare against with the method.
    * **The prompt engineering needed to generate results:** The paper depends on complex prompt engineering to get results from the LLM. This might restrict the scalability and accessibility of this framework.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The application of LLMs and evolutionary algorithms to traffic signal control has been shown to yield positive impacts.

Justification for the Score:

The paper presents a novel approach with practical relevance and demonstrable performance gains, but the limitations in scope and reliance on simulation warrant a cautious assessment. While the single intersection scope and the objective function being too simple significantly lower the importance of this paper, the introduction of LLMs to traffic signal control shows a potential for new ideas and breakthroughs.
Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### AI-Driven Drug Repurposing through miRNA-mRNA Relation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03336v1)
- **Authors**: Sharanya Manoharan, Balu Bhasuran, Oviya Ramalakshmi Iyyappan, Mohamed Saleem Abdul Shukkoor, Malathi Sellapan, Kalpana Raja
- **Abstract**: miRNA mRNA relations are closely linked to several biological processes and disease mechanisms In a recent study we tested the performance of large language models LLMs on extracting miRNA mRNA relations from PubMed PubMedBERT achieved the best performance of 0.783 F1 score for miRNA mRNA Interaction Corpus MMIC Here we first applied the finetuned PubMedBERT model to extract miRNA mRNA relations from PubMed for chronic obstructive pulmonary disease COPD Alzheimers disease AD stroke type 2 diabetes mellitus T2DM chronic liver disease and cancer Next we retrieved miRNA drug relations using KinderMiner a literature mining tool for relation extraction Then we constructed three interaction networks 1 disease centric network 2 drug centric network and 3 miRNA centric network comprising 3497 nodes and 16417 edges organized as a directed graph to capture complex biological relationships Finally we validated the drugs using MIMIC IV Our integrative approach revealed both established and novel candidate drugs for diseases under study through 595 miRNA drug relations extracted from PubMed To the best of our knowledge this is the first study to systematically extract and visualize relationships among four distinct biomedical entities miRNA mRNA drug and disease
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes an AI-driven drug repurposing approach by integrating miRNA-mRNA relationships with disease and drug data. The authors used a fine-tuned PubMedBERT model to extract miRNA-mRNA relations from PubMed abstracts for six chronic diseases: COPD, Alzheimer's disease (AD), stroke, type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM), chronic liver disease, and cancer. They then used KinderMiner to extract miRNA-drug relations.  These relations were used to construct three interaction networks: disease-centric, drug-centric, and miRNA-centric. Finally, the drugs identified were validated using the MIMIC-IV database.  The study identified both established and novel candidate drugs for the studied diseases, and visualized relationships among miRNA, mRNA, disease, and drug. The authors claim this is the first study to systematically extract and visualize these relationships among these four biomedical entities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The *claimed* novelty lies in the comprehensive, four-entity network analysis (miRNA, mRNA, disease, drug). While extracting miRNA-mRNA relations using NLP and applying drug repurposing are not entirely new, the integration of these four entities and the generation of the different networks present some novelty. The use of a previously fine-tuned PubMedBERT model is incremental; the main contribution is in the workflow and network construction.
*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from the potential to identify novel drug candidates for complex diseases.  By mapping the intricate relationships between miRNAs, mRNAs, drugs, and diseases, the study provides a resource for researchers interested in drug repurposing.  The validation using MIMIC-IV, although limited, adds credibility to the findings. However, the MIMIC-IV validation reveals primarily drugs already used in the studied diseases, limiting its claim to novel drug discoveries.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** Integrating multiple data sources (PubMed, KinderMiner, MIMIC-IV) and methods (NLP, network analysis) provides a holistic view of the relationships.
    *   **Network Visualization:** The creation of disease-centric, drug-centric, and miRNA-centric networks allows for exploring different perspectives on drug-disease relationships.
    *   **MIMIC-IV Validation:** Attempts to ground the findings in real-world clinical data.
    *   **Use of LLMs:** Leverages state-of-the-art NLP techniques for information extraction.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited MIMIC-IV Validation:** The MIMIC-IV validation identified drugs that were largely already being used to treat the targeted diseases. This weakens the "novel drug discovery" claims. The patient numbers and prescription frequencies are low for some drugs. The data on the MIMIC-IV databse has limitation that might reduce the statistical power of the valdiation step.
    *   **Reliance on Existing Literature:** The approach heavily relies on information present in PubMed and captured by KinderMiner. This means it may miss potential drug targets that are not well-documented in the literature or that exhibit indirect relationships.
    *   **Lack of Experimental Validation:** The study does not include any *in vitro* or *in vivo* experiments to validate the identified drug candidates. This is a significant limitation, as *in silico* predictions often do not translate to clinical efficacy.
    *   **KinderMiner Limitation:** Using KinderMiner identified a smaller amount of non-drug relations for miRNA. This may suggest this step has limitations.
    *   **LLM Specificity**: The use of a fine-tuned LLM may not be generalizable across other diseases.

*   **Impact:**

    *   The paper is valuable as a starting point for further research. The generated networks can be used as a resource for hypothesis generation.
    *   It could inform future experimental studies aimed at validating the identified drug candidates.
    *   The method could be adapted and applied to other diseases.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a reasonably novel approach to drug repurposing by integrating multiple data sources and analysis techniques. The study suffers from a lack of experimental validation, limiting the practical impact of the findings. While the comprehensive analysis and network visualization are strengths, the limited findings from the MIMIC-IV validation and strong dependence on existing literature diminishes the novelty and significance.

Score: 6

**Justification:**
A score of 6 reflects that this paper presents a potentially valuable, but primarily computational, study. The integrated approach and network analysis are interesting and contribute a new perspective, but the lack of experimental validation and limited positive outcomes from the validation with the MIMIC-IV severely restrict its significance and real-world impact. The dependency on the existing knowledge in PubMed and limitations in the validations using KinderMiner limit the novelty of the study. A high impact paper would require substantial experimental support for its predictions. While the paper has potential as a starting point for future research, its current state does not warrant a higher score.
- **Classification**: q-bio.MN
- **Score**: 6/10

### On the MIA Vulnerability Gap Between Private GANs and Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03341v1)
- **Authors**: Ilana Sebag, Jean-Yves Franceschi, Alain Rakotomamonjy, Alexandre Allauzen, Jamal Atif
- **Abstract**: Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) and diffusion models have emerged as leading approaches for high-quality image synthesis. While both can be trained under differential privacy (DP) to protect sensitive data, their sensitivity to membership inference attacks (MIAs), a key threat to data confidentiality, remains poorly understood. In this work, we present the first unified theoretical and empirical analysis of the privacy risks faced by differentially private generative models. We begin by showing, through a stability-based analysis, that GANs exhibit fundamentally lower sensitivity to data perturbations than diffusion models, suggesting a structural advantage in resisting MIAs. We then validate this insight with a comprehensive empirical study using a standardized MIA pipeline to evaluate privacy leakage across datasets and privacy budgets. Our results consistently reveal a marked privacy robustness gap in favor of GANs, even in strong DP regimes, highlighting that model type alone can critically shape privacy leakage.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the vulnerability to membership inference attacks (MIAs) in differentially private (DP) generative models, specifically comparing GANs and diffusion models.  The authors theoretically demonstrate and empirically validate that DP-diffusion models are more susceptible to MIAs than DP-GANs, even when trained under comparable privacy budgets. The theoretical analysis leverages uniform stability, showing that the training dynamics of diffusion models, particularly the weighted multi-pass denoising objective, lead to greater instability and increased leakage compared to GANs. The empirical study uses a shadow model-based MIA pipeline to confirm this vulnerability gap across datasets and privacy budgets. The study emphasizes that the model architecture itself plays a critical role in determining privacy risks under DP, alongside the privacy parameter ε.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's key novelty lies in providing a *unified theoretical and empirical comparison of MIA vulnerability between DP-GANs and DP-diffusion models*. While previous work has explored MIAs in generative models (both private and non-private) and differential privacy for generative models, this paper directly addresses the *relative* privacy risks between these two prominent architectures *under formal DP training*. The algorithmic stability analysis, applied to compare these two specific architectures under DP, is a valuable contribution. No existing works analyze how training procedures affect data leakage in the context of MIA across the architectures. It represents a critical step towards a more comprehensive understanding of the risk when deploying generative models.

**Significance:** The significance is moderate to high, given the increasing use of generative models in sensitive applications.  The finding that DP, quantified by epsilon, alone doesn't fully characterize privacy risk and that model architecture significantly influences leakage is a crucial takeaway. The paper's insights could inform the design of more robust DP-GANs and DP-diffusion models. Specifically, attention should be paid to architectural details when assessing MIAs.  The empirical validation supports the theoretical findings and also establishes a necessary standardized framework for this kind of evaluation in the space of DP generative models.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The research question is well-defined and relevant.
*   **Theoretical Rigor:** The paper provides a formal analysis using uniform stability, linking it to membership advantage.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The theoretical insights are supported by a comprehensive empirical study.
*   **Comparability:** The experimental setup ensures a fair comparison between GANs and diffusion models, using the same DP-SGD mechanism and privacy budget.
*   **Standardized evaluation pipeline:** The paper develops and standardizes the evaluation framework that will be used for the future comparative studies.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments focus primarily on the MNIST dataset. While valuable as a proof-of-concept, further evaluation on larger, more complex datasets (e.g., CIFAR-10, ImageNet-scale data) would strengthen the generalizability of the conclusions.  Generalizing from simple to complex data could reveal architectural specifics that impact MIA that cannot be observed in the small settings used in the paper.
*   **Limited Architectural Variation:** The GANs are adapted to be class conditional, however, the architectural variation inside both architectures remains limited. More exploration is required to understand how MIAs respond to these details.
*   **Black-Box Setting:** The MIA is evaluated in a black-box setting. Exploring white-box or grey-box attacks may provide additional insights into the underlying mechanisms driving the vulnerability gap.
*   **Practical implications:** The authors do not deeply discuss how the architecture can be improved to reduce the risk.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in differentially private generative modeling. It highlights the need to consider model architecture alongside epsilon when evaluating privacy risks and motivates the development of techniques to mitigate MIA vulnerability in diffusion models.  The standardized evaluation pipeline provides a benchmark for future work.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.  The paper has good novelty and significance and represents a substantial contribution in a specialized area. The theoretical analysis is sound, and the empirical validation provides concrete evidence for the vulnerability gap. However, the limited scope of the experiments, particularly the dataset and limited architectural variation, slightly reduces the impact. While the theoretical insight is important, the paper stops at a certain level of analysis, neglecting the practical implications, e.g., improvement strategies. Despite the weakness, the contribution of the paper is solid in terms of theoretical results, empirical findings and standardized MIA evaluation for DP generative models.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 0/10

### Language Models Do Not Follow Occam's Razor: A Benchmark for Inductive and Abductive Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03345v1)
- **Authors**: Yunxin Sun, Abulhair Saparov
- **Abstract**: Reasoning is a core capability in artificial intelligence systems, for which large language models (LLMs) have recently shown remarkable progress. However, most work focuses exclusively on deductive reasoning, which is problematic since other types of reasoning are also essential in solving real-world problems, and they are less explored. This work focuses on evaluating LLMs' inductive and abductive reasoning capabilities. We introduce a programmable and synthetic dataset, InAbHyD (pronounced in-a-bid), where each reasoning example consists of an incomplete world model and a set of observations. The task for the intelligent agent is to produce hypotheses to explain observations under the incomplete world model to solve each reasoning example. We propose a new metric to evaluate the quality of hypotheses based on Occam's Razor. We evaluate and analyze some state-of-the-art LLMs. Our analysis shows that LLMs can perform inductive and abductive reasoning in simple scenarios, but struggle with complex world models and producing high-quality hypotheses, even with popular reasoning-enhancing techniques such as in-context learning and RLVR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Language Models Do Not Follow Occam's Razor: A Benchmark for Inductive and Abductive Reasoning" introduces INABHYD, a synthetic benchmark for evaluating inductive and abductive reasoning capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs). The benchmark uses incomplete fictional world models (ontology trees) and observations, requiring LLMs to generate hypotheses that explain those observations, applying Occam's Razor (parsimony) to favor simpler explanations. The authors evaluated several state-of-the-art LLMs (LLAMA3-70B, GEMMA3-27B, DEEPSEEK-V3, GPT-40, and DEEPSEEK-R1-DISTILL-LLAMA-70B) and found that LLMs perform well in simple scenarios, but struggle with complex world models, generating high-quality parsimonious hypotheses, even when using reasoning-enhancing techniques like in-context learning or RLVR. The findings indicate that while LLMs exhibit some inductive and abductive capabilities, they don't inherently follow Occam's Razor and require further refinement for complex reasoning tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:
    *   **Focus on Inductive and Abductive Reasoning:** Prior research heavily emphasizes deductive reasoning in LLMs, while inductive and abductive reasoning remain relatively unexplored. This paper addresses that gap, making it a valuable contribution.
    *   **INABHYD Benchmark:** The creation of a synthetic and programmable dataset, INABHYD, is a significant step. It allows for controlled experimentation, mitigating the risks of training data contamination associated with real-world datasets. The ability to control the complexity of the world model (ontology tree height) is a strong feature. The evaluation metric factoring Occam's Razor is also innovative.
    *   **Evaluation of Existing Reasoning Techniques:** The assessment of techniques like in-context learning and RLVR for inductive and abductive reasoning is original and provides important insights for researchers.
    *   **Emphasis on hypothesis quality via occam's razor**: Previous work on evaluating abductive and inductive reasoning abilities did not evaluate hypothesis quality based on parsimony/occams razor.

*   **Significance:**
    *   **Identifying Limitations:** The paper clearly highlights the limitations of current LLMs in complex inductive and abductive reasoning scenarios. This understanding is crucial for directing future research efforts.
    *   **Practical Implications:** Scientific discovery is mentioned as an area that will need occam's razor for successful integration of LLMs, and this is a key practical area that will rely on high quality abductive and inductive reasoning.
    *   **Methodological Contribution:** INABHYD can serve as a valuable resource for the research community, enabling further investigation and development of improved reasoning capabilities in LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly defines the scope of its investigation and the specific types of reasoning it aims to evaluate.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The methodology is well-structured, starting from the generation of a synthetic dataset, explanation of the test framework, to conducting experiments, and analyzing the results.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper offers a comprehensive analysis of the results, considering various factors like the complexity of the world model, the number of hypotheses, and the effectiveness of reasoning-enhancing techniques.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, with clear explanations of the methodology, results, and analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Fictional World Models:** While the use of fictional world models mitigates data contamination, it also raises questions about the generalizability of the results to real-world scenarios. The jump from fictional ontology trees to complex scientific or medical domains might be significant.
    *   **Expressiveness of FOL:** The choice of first-order logic (FOL) as the basis for world models is a limitation, as it constrains the complexity and expressiveness of the reasoning examples. Moving to higher-order logic, though challenging, might be necessary for more realistic scenarios.
    *   **Limited Scope of Hypotheses:** The restriction to hypotheses expressible in first-order logic limits the scope of potential explanations LLMs can generate.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in LLMs, shifting focus from purely deductive reasoning to more complex forms of reasoning like induction and abduction. It also encourages the development of new techniques and architectures that can better handle uncertainty, incompleteness, and the principle of parsimony. It pushes the need for explainability of reasoning by looking at the step-by-step proofs, which is not considered by other works in the inductive and abductive reasoning space.

*   **Room for Improvement:**
    *   **Real-World Applications:** Future work should explore how to bridge the gap between synthetic datasets and real-world applications, potentially by incorporating real-world knowledge or developing transfer learning techniques.
    *   **More Complex Reasoning:** The authors could explore more complex forms of reasoning, such as causal reasoning or analogical reasoning, building upon the foundation laid by this paper.
    *   **Addressing Error Types:** The common error types identified in Section 4.5 could be addressed by incorporating them into a targeted training regime, to help LLMs learn and avoid making those errors in the future.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by addressing a relatively under-explored area of inductive and abductive reasoning in LLMs. The introduction of the INABHYD benchmark and the associated evaluation metric are valuable assets for the research community. The systematic analysis of LLMs and existing reasoning techniques provides important insights. However, the reliance on fictional world models and the limitations of FOL restrict the generalizability and scope of the findings. The paper also does a great job connecting results and observations and proposing future works. The score reflects the paper's novelty, significance, and methodological rigor, tempered by the limitations in scope and potential generalizability. A higher score would require addressing some of these limitations, such as incorporating real-world knowledge or exploring more complex reasoning forms.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### TinyDrop: Tiny Model Guided Token Dropping for Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03379v1)
- **Authors**: Guoxin Wang, Qingyuan Wang, Binhua Huang, Shaowu Chen, Deepu John
- **Abstract**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) achieve strong performance in image classification but incur high computational costs from processing all image tokens. To reduce inference costs in large ViTs without compromising accuracy, we propose TinyDrop, a training-free token dropping framework guided by a lightweight vision model. The guidance model estimates the importance of tokens while performing inference, thereby selectively discarding low-importance tokens if large vit models need to perform attention calculations. The framework operates plug-and-play, requires no architectural modifications, and is compatible with diverse ViT architectures. Evaluations on standard image classification benchmarks demonstrate that our framework reduces FLOPs by up to 80% for ViTs with minimal accuracy degradation, highlighting its generalization capability and practical utility for efficient ViT-based classification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TinyDrop, a training-free framework for reducing the computational cost of Vision Transformers (ViTs). TinyDrop utilizes a lightweight "guidance model" (a smaller vision model) to estimate the importance of individual tokens (image patches). Based on these importance scores, it selectively discards less important tokens before they enter the main ViT's self-attention layers. This reduces the sequence length that the ViT needs to process, leading to lower FLOPs.  The approach is designed to be plug-and-play, requiring no fine-tuning of the target ViT and being compatible with different ViT architectures. It also incorporates an early exit mechanism where the guidance model may directly classify the image if its confidence is high enough, further reducing computational cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using a smaller model to guide token dropping isn't entirely novel. Previous works like PaPr also use lightweight networks to guide patch selection. However, TinyDrop's focus on a completely training-free approach, combined with the confidence-based early exit and a more comprehensive treatment of positional embeddings, adds a degree of novelty. The adaptive confidence-to-drop mapping function to adjust the drop rate is a nice touch. The authors should have provided a more detailed comparison of their framework's novelty compared to prior works.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in ViTs: their high computational cost. Making ViTs more efficient is essential for their deployment in resource-constrained environments. The significant FLOPs reduction (up to 80%) with minimal accuracy loss is a strong result and showcases the potential of the approach.  The method's ease of integration (plug-and-play) is a significant advantage. The reported gains on various ViT architectures boost the significance and utility of the approach.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Training-free:** The most significant strength is the training-free nature of the method. This makes it directly applicable to existing pre-trained ViT models without requiring additional fine-tuning, a significant advantage in practical scenarios.
    * **Plug-and-play Compatibility:**  The framework's modularity allows for easy integration with various ViT architectures and different guidance models, enhancing its practicality and wider applicability.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results on standard image classification benchmarks, demonstrating substantial FLOPs reductions with minimal accuracy loss.
    * **Early Exit Mechanism:** The inclusion of an early exit strategy based on the guidance model's confidence further enhances efficiency.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Guidance Model Overhead:** While the authors mention the FLOPs of the guidance model, a more detailed analysis of the latency overhead introduced by the guidance model and the resizing operations is needed.  The guidance model is still an added cost, especially in latency-sensitive applications.
    * **Limited Ablation Studies:**  While the ablation study on the curvature parameter γ is insightful, further ablation studies could explore the impact of different guidance model architectures on performance. They should also consider different image resolutions for input to the guidance model and how that impacts accuracy and overhead.
    * **Dependence on Guidance Model Accuracy:** The performance of TinyDrop relies heavily on the accuracy and quality of the guidance model. A poor guidance model could lead to suboptimal token selection and accuracy degradation.
    * **Scalability:** The results are primarily focused on ImageNet-1K. Analyzing the performance on larger and more complex datasets would further solidify the framework's scalability and generalizability.
* **Impact:** TinyDrop has the potential to make ViTs more accessible and deployable in various real-world applications, especially where computational resources are limited. The training-free nature is a strong selling point.

**Justification for Score:**

While the idea of using a smaller model for guidance isn't entirely groundbreaking, the authors present a well-engineered, training-free framework with strong empirical results. The plug-and-play nature of the approach and its significant FLOPs reduction make it a valuable contribution to the field of efficient ViT inference.  However, the dependence on guidance model accuracy, the need for more detailed latency analysis, and the limited exploration of guidance model architectures hold it back from a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### More Parameters Than Populations: A Systematic Literature Review of Large Language Models within Survey Research
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03391v1)
- **Authors**: Trent D. Buskirk, Florian Keusch, Leah von der Heyde, Adam Eck
- **Abstract**: Survey research has a long-standing history of being a human-powered field, but one that embraces various technologies for the collection, processing, and analysis of various behavioral, political, and social outcomes of interest, among others. At the same time, Large Language Models (LLMs) bring new technological challenges and prerequisites in order to fully harness their potential. In this paper, we report work-in-progress on a systematic literature review based on keyword searches from multiple large-scale databases as well as citation networks that assesses how LLMs are currently being applied within the survey research process. We synthesize and organize our findings according to the survey research process to include examples of LLM usage across three broad phases: pre-data collection, data collection, and post-data collection. We discuss selected examples of potential use cases for LLMs as well as its pitfalls based on examples from existing literature. Considering survey research has rich experience and history regarding data quality, we discuss some opportunities and describe future outlooks for survey research to contribute to the continued development and refinement of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents work-in-progress on a systematic literature review examining the applications of Large Language Models (LLMs) within survey research.  It categorizes LLM usage across three phases of the survey research pipeline: pre-data collection, data collection, and post-data collection. The authors synthesize examples from empirical and theoretical work, highlighting use cases like instrument development, synthetic respondent modeling, and automated text classification. The paper also discusses potential pitfalls of using LLMs, like hallucinated responses or misclassification, emphasizing the importance of data quality and representational accuracy.  Finally, the authors discuss that while they focused on the ways LLMs are used within survey research, some of the excluded papers explored how survey research could also improve LLMs. The paper concludes by pointing out the uneven distribution of LLM applications across the survey pipeline, the need for more research on core practices like live interviewing and cross-lingual adaptation, and the importance of considering methodological and ethical implications for effective LLM integration.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic approach to reviewing the existing literature on LLM applications in survey research. While individual papers have explored specific LLM applications, this paper attempts to provide a broader, organized overview based on the survey research process. This synthesis is valuable for understanding the current landscape and identifying gaps. Jansen et al. (2023) provided largely a conceptual overview of the potential uses and considerations for incorporating LLMs within the survey research context, which makes this paper an improvement as it transitions from an ideation phase to an implementation phase.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. It's a timely exploration, given the rapid evolution of LLMs and their increasing application in various fields. By organizing the existing literature and highlighting both opportunities and challenges, the paper provides a valuable resource for survey researchers interested in leveraging LLMs. It's particularly significant because it raises crucial questions about data quality, potential biases, and ethical considerations related to the use of LLMs in survey research, which are often overlooked in the enthusiasm surrounding these technologies. The paper is still in progress, which limits the analysis currently. However, further systematic review with a more robust approach will make this work more significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic approach: Provides a structured overview of LLM applications in survey research.
    *   Emphasis on the survey process: Categorizing applications by phase makes the review practically relevant for researchers.
    *   Balanced perspective: Acknowledges both the opportunities and the potential pitfalls.
    *   Highlights ethical considerations: Addresses the importance of data quality, biases, and representational accuracy.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Work-in-progress: The paper acknowledges being preliminary, which limits the depth of analysis.
    *   The figures are difficult to read, for instance, Figure 1 is small, and it's difficult to read some of the labels. In Figure 2(a), the color choices make it difficult to differentiate between data collection and post-data collection.
    *   Limited scope: While the systematic approach is a strength, the paper does not get deep into the specifics of the papers found.
    *   Lack of concrete recommendations: While the paper raises important questions, it doesn't offer specific recommendations or best practices for researchers using LLMs.
    *   The description of the selection criteria seems insufficient. Additional information could be added to the appendix.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the paper's novelty in providing a systematic overview, its moderate significance due to the timely exploration and balanced perspective, and its identified strengths and weaknesses, I assign it a score of **6**. It's a useful contribution to the field, organizing existing knowledge and raising important questions. However, its preliminary nature and lack of concrete recommendations limit its immediate impact. Future iterations with a more robust approach will significantly enhance its value and potential influence.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.DL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Curse of Knowledge: When Complex Evaluation Context Benefits yet Biases LLM Judges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03419v1)
- **Authors**: Weiyuan Li, Xintao Wang, Siyu Yuan, Rui Xu, Jiangjie Chen, Qingqing Dong, Yanghua Xiao, Deqing Yang
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) grow more capable, they face increasingly diverse and complex tasks, making reliable evaluation challenging. The paradigm of LLMs as judges has emerged as a scalable solution, yet prior work primarily focuses on simple settings. Their reliability in complex tasks--where multi-faceted rubrics, unstructured reference answers, and nuanced criteria are critical--remains understudied. In this paper, we constructed ComplexEval, a challenge benchmark designed to systematically expose and quantify Auxiliary Information Induced Biases. We systematically investigated and validated 6 previously unexplored biases across 12 basic and 3 advanced scenarios. Key findings reveal: (1) all evaluated models exhibit significant susceptibility to these biases, with bias magnitude scaling with task complexity; (2) notably, Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) show paradoxical vulnerability. Our in-depth analysis offers crucial insights for improving the accuracy and verifiability of evaluation signals, paving the way for more general and robust evaluation models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Curse of Knowledge: When Complex Evaluation Context Benefits yet Biases LLM Judges" explores the biases introduced when using LLMs as judges in complex evaluation tasks. The authors argue that while auxiliary information (references, rubrics, background knowledge) is meant to improve the reliability and verifiability of LLM-based evaluation, it can also introduce new and significant biases. To investigate this, they construct a benchmark called COMPLEXEVAL, which consists of two parts: COMPLEXEVAL-Basic and COMPLEXEVAL-Advanced. They systematically investigate several previously unexplored biases across different scenarios, finding that all models tested are susceptible to these biases, with reasoning models showing a paradoxical vulnerability. They identify specific bias patterns and provide insights for improving the accuracy and robustness of LLM-based evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses an important and under-explored area: the biases introduced by auxiliary information when LLMs are used as judges in complex tasks. While prior work has examined various evaluation biases in LLMs, this paper makes a novel contribution by focusing specifically on the interaction between task complexity, auxiliary information, and resulting biases. The COMPLEXEVAL benchmark is a valuable contribution to the field, offering a systematic way to study these biases.

*   **Significance:** The use of LLMs as judges is becoming increasingly prevalent, particularly in areas like reinforcement learning. This paper raises critical concerns about the reliability of these evaluations in complex scenarios. Identifying and quantifying these biases is essential for improving the trustworthiness of LLM-based evaluations and preventing their unintended consequences in applications like reward modeling. The paper’s finding that stronger reasoning models can be *more* susceptible to these biases is particularly significant and warrants further research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies and frames the problem of auxiliary information-induced biases.
    *   **Systematic methodology:** The construction of COMPLEXEVAL allows for a systematic and controlled study of the biases.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims, with detailed analysis of model performance and bias patterns.
    *   **Actionable insights:** The identified bias patterns and insights offer a roadmap for improving LLM-based evaluation models.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework**: The ComplexEval framework created in this paper is a solid step forward in evaluating how LLMs perform when auxiliary data is introduced.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope:** The paper primarily focuses on biases induced by references, rubrics, and background knowledge. Other forms of auxiliary information and evaluation contexts may exist, requiring further investigation.
    *   **Mitigation strategies:** While the paper identifies the problem and offers some initial directions, it doesn't provide concrete and fully developed mitigation strategies. Future work could focus on developing and evaluating specific techniques for reducing these biases.
    *   **Some of the experiments can feel somewhat synthetic**: Although comprehensive, some of the auxiliary information used in the datasets may not directly mirror the auxiliary data humans would use in equivalent contexts.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field by raising awareness of these often-overlooked biases. It can encourage the development of more robust and reliable LLM-based evaluation techniques. Also, future research can leverage the COMPLEXEVAL benchmark and the identified bias patterns to explore new mitigation strategies.

Overall, the paper is a well-executed and significant contribution to the field of LLM evaluation. It provides a valuable benchmark, identifies key biases, and offers actionable insights for improving the trustworthiness of LLM judges in complex tasks.

### sam-llm: interpretable lane change trajectoryprediction via parametric finetuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03462v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuo Cao, Yunxiao Shi, Min Xu
- **Abstract**: This work introduces SAM-LLM, a novel hybrid architecture that bridges the gap between the contextual reasoning of Large Language Models (LLMs) and the physical precision of kinematic lane change models for autonomous driving. The system is designed for interpretable lane change trajectory prediction by finetuning an LLM to output the core physical parameters of a trajectory model instead of raw coordinates. For lane-keeping scenarios, the model predicts discrete coordinates, but for lane change maneuvers, it generates the parameters for an enhanced Sinusoidal Acceleration Model (SAM), including lateral displacement, maneuver duration, initial lateral velocity, and longitudinal velocity change. This parametric approach yields a complete, continuous, and physically plausible trajectory model that is inherently interpretable and computationally efficient, achieving an 80% reduction in output size compared to coordinate-based methods. The SAM-LLM achieves a state-of-the-art overall intention prediction accuracy of 98.73%, demonstrating performance equivalent to traditional LLM predictors while offering significant advantages in explainability and resource efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SAM-LLM, a novel hybrid architecture designed to improve lane change trajectory prediction by combining the contextual reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) with the physical precision of kinematic models. Instead of directly predicting coordinates, the LLM is fine-tuned to output parameters for a Sinusoidal Acceleration Model (SAM). This parametric approach allows for complete, continuous, and physically interpretable trajectories while also being computationally efficient. The system uses a hybrid strategy, making coordinate-based predictions for lane-keeping scenarios. The authors claim state-of-the-art intention prediction accuracy and significant computational efficiency gains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using an LLM to predict *parameters* of a kinematic model instead of raw coordinates is a significant step forward. This allows for a more physically grounded and interpretable prediction than typical coordinate-based trajectory predictions from LLMs. The integration of CoT prompting to enhance explainability is also a valuable addition, even if not entirely novel in itself. The hybrid approach combining coordinate and parameter prediction is also a novel and practical element, focusing the benefits of both techniques where most useful.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is substantial. By producing interpretable trajectories, SAM-LLM enables better analysis of driving behavior. The computational efficiency gains reported are also practically important for real-time autonomous driving applications. The potential for generating complete, smooth trajectories beyond discrete prediction horizons could improve safety and planning. However, its significance relies on its robustness in more diverse scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of parametric modeling for trajectory prediction addresses a key limitation of coordinate-based methods.
    *   The hybrid architecture is well-motivated and cleverly combines different approaches.
    *   The CoT reasoning enhances explainability.
    *   The paper reports impressive intention prediction accuracy.
    *   The claimed reduction in output dimensionality and inference speedup is a significant advantage.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation relies heavily on the highD dataset. It is important to evaluate it on more diverse datasets or simulated scenarios.
    *   While interpretability is claimed, the evaluation relies heavily on quantitative metrics. A more detailed qualitative analysis of the CoT reasoning would be beneficial.
    *   The limitations of SAM should be acknowledged. How well does it capture more complex or unusual lane change maneuvers?
    *   The paper could include more discussion and comparison to other recent approaches to explainable trajectory prediction beyond LC-LLM to better highlight the advantages of the approach.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    SAM-LLM represents a significant advancement in LLM-based lane change trajectory prediction. Its innovation lies in its hybrid parametric approach that enhances interpretability, and demonstrates clear benefits over existing LLM-based solutions. While some improvements can be made in validation and qualitative analyses, the core contribution is strong.

### The Impact of Critique on LLM-Based Model Generation from Natural Language: The Case of Activity Diagrams
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03463v1)
- **Authors**: Parham Khamsepour, Mark Cole, Ish Ashraf, Sandeep Puri, Mehrdad Sabetzadeh, Shiva Nejati
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) show strong potential for automating the generation of models from natural-language descriptions. A common approach is an iterative generate-critique-refine loop, where candidate models are produced, evaluated, and updated based on detected issues. This process needs to address: (1) structural correctness - compliance with well-formedness rules - and (2) semantic alignment - accurate reflection of the intended meaning in the source text. We present LADEX (LLM-based Activity Diagram Extractor), a pipeline for deriving activity diagrams from natural-language process descriptions using an LLM-driven critique-refine process. Structural checks in LADEX can be performed either algorithmically or by an LLM, while alignment checks are always performed by an LLM. We design five ablated variants of LADEX to study: (i) the impact of the critique-refine loop itself, (ii) the role of LLM-based semantic checks, and (iii) the comparative effectiveness of algorithmic versus LLM-based structural checks. To evaluate LADEX, we compare the generated activity diagrams with expert-created ground truths using trace-based operational semantics. This enables automated measurement of correctness and completeness. Experiments on two datasets indicate that: (1) the critique-refine loop improves structural validity, correctness, and completeness compared to single-pass generation; (2) algorithmic structural checks eliminate inconsistencies that LLM-based checks fail to detect, improving correctness by an average of 17.81% and completeness by 13.24% over LLM-only checks; and (3) combining algorithmic structural checks with LLM-based semantic checks, implemented using the reasoning-focused O4 Mini, achieves the best overall performance - yielding average correctness of up to 86.37% and average completeness of up to 88.56% - while requiring fewer than five LLM calls on average.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents LADEX, an LLM-based pipeline designed to generate activity diagrams from natural language descriptions of processes. LADEX uses an iterative "critique-refine" loop where candidate diagrams are generated, evaluated for structural correctness and semantic alignment with the source text, and then refined. The paper explores different ways to implement the critique step, including algorithmic and LLM-based checks for structural validity, and examines the impact of these different strategies on the quality (correctness, completeness) and cost (LLM calls) of the generated diagrams. Experiments are conducted on a public dataset (PAGED) and an industrial dataset, using different LLMs. The results suggest that combining algorithmic structural checks with LLM-based semantic checks in the critique-refine loop yields the best overall performance, producing structurally sound, semantically accurate, and complete activity diagrams.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic exploration of the critique-refine loop for generating behavioral models from natural language, specifically focusing on activity diagrams.  Prior work has touched on LLM-based model generation, but this paper provides a deeper dive into the trade-offs involved in the critique stage, comparing algorithmic vs. LLM-based structural checks and considering the impact of alignment checks.  The use of trace-based operational semantics for automated correctness and completeness measurement is a valuable contribution and is more rigorous than previous studies, that often rely on manual evaluation.

*   **Significance:** The findings have practical significance for the automated generation of software models.  The conclusion that algorithmic structural checks outperform LLM-based structural checks in this context is important, as it highlights the continued value of formal methods even in the age of LLMs. The recommendation to combine algorithmic structural checks with LLM-based semantic checks provides a concrete guideline for building effective model generation pipelines. Furthermore, the comprehensive evaluation metrics presented in the paper provide a benchmark for future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper conducts a thorough experimental evaluation, comparing different configurations of LADEX on two datasets with multiple LLMs.
    *   **Rigorous Metrics:** The use of trace-based operational semantics provides a well-defined and automatable way to measure the correctness and completeness of generated models.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper provides practical recommendations for the design of LLM-based model generation pipelines.
    *   **Addressing a Gap:**  The work addresses a gap in the existing literature by systematically studying the impact of different types of checks within a critique-refine framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Models:** While activity diagrams are important, the findings may not generalize directly to other types of models or more complex modeling notations. The reliance on a specific CSV format for Draw.io is somewhat limiting.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** Although two datasets were used, the results still could be specific to the characteristics of those datasets. The superior results seen with the proposed method might not carry over to scenarios that differ significantly from the chosen test cases.
    *   **Cost Considerations:** The evaluation primarily focuses on the *number* of LLM calls. While relevant, the actual monetary cost or time taken per call is not fully accounted for, which can impact the true viability of different strategies given LLM pricing dynamics.
    *   **LLM Selection:** While the authors used a variety of LLMs, the selection criteria for these LLMs is not entirely justified. Future research could explore a broader range of LLMs with varying architectures and training datasets to ensure that the findings are robust across different LLM capabilities.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the area of automated model generation, particularly regarding the design of critique-refine loops and the use of formal methods alongside LLMs. The rigorous evaluation methodology could be adopted by other researchers in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by systematically examining different aspects of LLM-based model generation, particularly the critique-refine loop. While the approach may not be broadly applicable across all domains and types of models, the techniques to combine formal verification techniques with LLM-based generation to improve the results are of broad applicability and likely to stimulate future work in this area.  The use of trace-based operational semantics for evaluation strengthens the findings. However, the limited scope of the models evaluated and the dataset dependency limit the impact to a degree. The potential influence on the field and the rigorous evaluation methodology are key strengths.

### Parameter-Efficient Adaptation of mPLUG-Owl2 via Pixel-Level Visual Prompts for NR-IQA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03494v1)
- **Authors**: Yahya Benmahane, Mohammed El Hassouni
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we propose a novel parameter-efficient adaptation method for No- Reference Image Quality Assessment (NR-IQA) using visual prompts optimized in pixel-space. Unlike full fine-tuning of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), our approach trains only 600K parameters at most (< 0.01% of the base model), while keeping the underlying model fully frozen. During inference, these visual prompts are combined with images via addition and processed by mPLUG-Owl2 with the textual query "Rate the technical quality of the image." Evaluations across distortion types (synthetic, realistic, AI-generated) on KADID- 10k, KonIQ-10k, and AGIQA-3k demonstrate competitive performance against full finetuned methods and specialized NR-IQA models, achieving 0.93 SRCC on KADID-10k. To our knowledge, this is the first work to leverage pixel-space visual prompts for NR-IQA, enabling efficient MLLM adaptation for low-level vision tasks. The source code is publicly available at https: // github. com/ yahya-ben/ mplug2-vp-for-nriqa .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a parameter-efficient method for adapting Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) like mPLUG-Owl2 for No-Reference Image Quality Assessment (NR-IQA).  Instead of fully fine-tuning the entire MLLM, the method learns visual prompts – optimized pixel patterns added to the input image – to guide the MLLM's quality assessment.  The approach freezes the base model and trains only a small number of parameters associated with the visual prompt.  The adapted model is then queried with a textual prompt like "Rate the technical quality of the image". Experiments on synthetic, realistic, and AI-generated image datasets demonstrate competitive performance compared to fully fine-tuned models and specialized NR-IQA architectures, while using significantly fewer trainable parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the application of visual prompting in pixel-space to adapt MLLMs for NR-IQA.  Prior work has explored visual prompting for other vision tasks (image classification) and has investigated textual prompting and full finetuning for MLLMs in NR-IQA. However, combining pixel-space visual prompting with a frozen MLLM architecture for NR-IQA is a relatively unexplored area. While visual prompting as a concept isn't new, its specific implementation for this task and with this architecture constitutes a significant contribution.
*   **Significance:** Parameter-efficient adaptation of large models is crucial for practical deployment, especially in resource-constrained environments. Reducing the trainable parameters to a fraction of the full model size makes adaptation more feasible. The method's competitive performance relative to fully fine-tuned models showcases its potential. Furthermore, by only changing the image representation going into the model, the method sidesteps the risk of model forgetting that can occur with full fine tuning. The experiments demonstrating effectiveness across different distortion types (synthetic, realistic, and AI-generated) increase the method's practical relevance. However, a gap with existing fully finetuned models suggests that prompt tuning may still need more work to reach full potential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Parameter Efficiency:** A key strength is the substantial reduction in trainable parameters, making it a practical approach for adapting large models.
    *   **Competitive Performance:**  The method achieves performance comparable to or exceeding that of fully fine-tuned or specialized NR-IQA models, despite its parameter efficiency.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a clear description of the approach, including the visual prompt configurations, training details, and evaluation metrics.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** Experiments are conducted on multiple datasets covering diverse distortion types.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Performance Gap:** The proposed method still shows a performance gap compared with more specialized NR-IQA models in some evaluations.
    *   **Textual Prompting is Fixed:** The method only tunes the visual prompt, leaving the textual prompt untouched. While a design choice, there may be additional performance to be gained by co-tuning the textual prompt.
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:**  More detailed ablation studies examining the impact of different visual prompt configurations and sizes could provide further insights.

*   **Impact:** This paper contributes to the growing body of research on adapting MLLMs for specific vision tasks. It offers a practical approach to NR-IQA using visual prompts, potentially influencing future research in this area. The use of pixel-level prompts for MLLMs could be extended to other low-level vision problems.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel application of visual prompting to NR-IQA, demonstrating its effectiveness for parameter-efficient adaptation of MLLMs. While some performance gaps exist and could be investigated through extended ablation studies or co-tuning of textual prompts, the benefits for memory constraints are significant. This paper marks a step in a relatively unexplored direction and provides insight on how visual prompts can be used for a traditionally hard problem.

### OneCAT: Decoder-Only Auto-Regressive Model for Unified Understanding and Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03498v1)
- **Authors**: Han Li, Xinyu Peng, Yaoming Wang, Zelin Peng, Xin Chen, Rongxiang Weng, Jingang Wang, Xunliang Cai, Wenrui Dai, Hongkai Xiong
- **Abstract**: We introduce OneCAT, a unified multimodal model that seamlessly integrates understanding, generation, and editing within a novel, pure decoder-only transformer architecture. Our framework uniquely eliminates the need for external components such as Vision Transformers (ViT) or vision tokenizer during inference, leading to significant efficiency gains, especially for high-resolution inputs. This is achieved through a modality-specific Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) structure trained with a single autoregressive (AR) objective, which also natively supports dynamic resolutions. Furthermore, we pioneer a multi-scale visual autoregressive mechanism within the Large Language Model (LLM) that drastically reduces decoding steps compared to diffusion-based methods while maintaining state-of-the-art performance. Our findings demonstrate the powerful potential of pure autoregressive modeling as a sufficient and elegant foundation for unified multimodal intelligence. As a result, OneCAT sets a new performance standard, outperforming existing open-source unified multimodal models across benchmarks for multimodal generation, editing, and understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OneCAT: Decoder-Only Auto-Regressive Model for Unified Understanding and Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OneCAT, a novel, pure decoder-only transformer architecture designed for unified multimodal understanding, generation, and editing.  A key aspect of OneCAT is the elimination of external components like Vision Transformers (ViTs) or vision tokenizers during inference, purportedly leading to efficiency gains, especially for high-resolution inputs. This is achieved via a modality-specific Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) structure trained with a single autoregressive (AR) objective, natively supporting dynamic resolutions. The authors also propose a multi-scale visual autoregressive mechanism within the LLM that aims to reduce decoding steps compared to diffusion-based methods while maintaining state-of-the-art performance. The paper claims that OneCAT achieves new performance standards, outperforming existing open-source unified multimodal models on various benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of a pure decoder-only architecture for *unified* multimodal tasks has been explored before, such as Fuyu-8B. The claimed novelty primarily lies in the specific architectural choices: the modality-specific MoE structure and the multi-scale visual autoregressive mechanism *within* the LLM (as opposed to external components). The combination of these elements is potentially novel. The absence of a tokenizer is also claimed, which could be important. However, these architectural details must be truly groundbreaking to justify a high significance score. From the details provided, it is *unclear* if these components substantially differ from existing solutions. A key issue to examine is whether the benefits of removing the vision encoder outweigh the information loss or difficulties in representing complex visual data directly.
*   **Significance:** If the claims of superior performance, especially regarding inference efficiency and high-resolution image handling, are substantiated, the paper could be significant. Efficiency is crucial for practical deployment.  The promise of a unified model capable of understanding, generation, and editing is also compelling. However, the *true* significance hinges on empirical results and comparisons with other state-of-the-art models. The paper states that it outperforms "existing open-source unified multimodal models," which is a limited claim. Crucially, the claimed performance is achieved while being a *pure* decoder, which could be a significant factor for future architectures.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The decoder-only approach is appealing due to its architectural simplicity and potential for efficiency.
    *   The unified objective and avoidance of complex pipelines are beneficial for training and inference.
    *   The claimed improvements in inference speed, especially for high-resolution inputs, would be a valuable contribution.
    *   The ability to handle dynamic resolutions is practically useful.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper needs to rigorously prove performance gains. Quantitative results must be thoroughly presented and compared to the *best* available models, not just open-source ones.  Ablation studies dissecting the contributions of the MoE structure and multi-scale autoregressive mechanism are vital.
    *   The limitations of the approach must be clearly discussed. What types of visual data or tasks does OneCAT struggle with? How does the pure decoder architecture impact performance on tasks that traditionally benefit from strong visual encoders (e.g., fine-grained visual recognition)?
    *   The explanation of the MoE implementation and multi-scale autoregressive mechanism needs more clarity.
    *   The comparison with other unified models should be more thorough, analyzing their specific design choices and trade-offs.
*   **Potential Influence:** If OneCAT truly delivers on its promises, it could influence the direction of multimodal modeling towards simpler, more efficient, and unified architectures.  It could serve as a strong baseline and inspire further research in decoder-only models. The emphasis on inference efficiency is particularly timely.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

Given the potential but unproven advantages, and considering the existing body of work on decoder-only multimodal models and multi-scale generation, I assign a score of **6**. The idea is interesting, but the current paper needs substantially stronger empirical evidence and a more rigorous analysis of its benefits and limitations to justify a higher score. *Without more concrete evidence of a major breakthrough*, this contribution is incremental at best. The lack of detailed comparison against all state-of-the-art models is also a concern, as is the need for more explanation regarding internal mechanisms. Therefore, given its novelty and potential, a score of 6 appropriately reflects its *current* impact, which is moderately positive, awaiting further validation.
