### Divergence-Kernel method for linear responses and diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03992v1)
- **Authors**: Angxiu Ni
- **Abstract**: We derive the divergence-kernel formula for the linear response (parameter-derivative of marginal or stationary distributions) of random dynamical systems, and formally pass to the continuous-time limit. Our formula works for multiplicative and parameterized noise over any period of time; it does not require hyperbolicity. Then we derive a pathwise Monte-Carlo algorithm for linear responses. With this, we propose a forward-only diffusion generative model and test on simple problems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a divergence-kernel method for computing linear responses (parameter derivatives of marginal distributions) in random dynamical systems. The method is extended to continuous-time limits, resulting in a formula applicable to Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs).  A key advantage is that the method handles multiplicative and parameterized noise without requiring hyperbolicity. The paper proposes a pathwise Monte-Carlo algorithm for computing linear responses and uses it to develop a forward-only diffusion generative model.  Numerical examples, including a 1-D process with multiplicative noise and a 40-dimensional Lorenz 96 model, are presented to demonstrate the method's efficacy.  The paper argues that this new method offers advantages over existing techniques like path-perturbation and kernel-differentiation methods. Finally, it introduces a simple forward-only diffusion model and uses the new method to optimize the diffusion process to match a target distribution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents a new formulation for linear response calculation, specifically targeting SDEs with multiplicative noise. The divergence-kernel approach itself builds on the author's previous work, but the extension to linear responses and the combination with forward-only diffusion models represents a significant step forward.

*   **Strengths:** The core novelty lies in the *combination* of the divergence-kernel framework with the linear response problem for SDEs *and* the application to building a new diffusion model paradigm. The ability to handle multiplicative noise without strong hyperbolicity assumptions is also a strong selling point compared to previous approaches.
*   **Weaknesses:**  While the core idea is sound, the paper heavily relies on *formal* arguments, particularly in the transition to continuous time. This means the rigorous mathematical justification is lacking, potentially limiting its immediate practical applicability in situations where theoretical guarantees are essential.  Additionally, the examples presented, while demonstrating the method, are still relatively simple.  A more complex and realistic test case would significantly strengthen the paper's claims.

**Significance:**

The potential significance of this work stems from its ability to address limitations of existing methods for linear response calculations and diffusion modeling.  If fully validated, the approach could have a notable impact on various fields, including:

*   **Sensitivity analysis of complex systems:**  The method's ability to handle multiplicative noise makes it potentially valuable for analyzing the sensitivity of systems where noise plays a crucial role (e.g., climate models, financial systems).
*   **Generative modeling:** The proposed forward-only diffusion model is an interesting alternative to current approaches, potentially offering improved efficiency and control over the generative process.
*   **Optimization of stochastic systems:**  The gradient-based optimization capabilities open doors for directly optimizing stochastic systems with respect to diffusion coefficients, which was previously challenging.

However, several factors temper this potential impact:

*   **Formal derivation:** The lack of rigorous mathematical justification introduces uncertainty about the method's reliability and convergence properties in more complex scenarios. This significantly *reduces* its immediate adoption and influence.
*   **Computational cost:**  While claimed to have advantages, the computational complexity of the divergence kernel method compared to other techniques still requires thorough benchmarking and analysis. It needs to be shown to be *demonstrably* faster or more accurate in real-world problems to have significant practical impact.
*   **Limited empirical validation:** The numerical examples presented are preliminary. More extensive testing and comparisons with existing methods on benchmark problems are necessary to fully assess its performance and limitations.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates *potential* and shows a promising new approach. However, the lack of rigorous mathematical justification and limited empirical validation prevent it from being a truly significant contribution *at this stage*. Its claims need to be thoroughly proven mathematically and computationally. It provides a step in the right direction by combining the divergence-kernel method to the linear response problem, but rigorous validation of the benefits of this method is yet to be seen.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: math.DS
- **Score**: 5/10

### RTQA : Recursive Thinking for Complex Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.03995v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaoyan Gong, Juan Li, Zhiqiang Liu, Lei Liang, Huajun Chen, Wen Zhang
- **Abstract**: Current temporal knowledge graph question answering (TKGQA) methods primarily focus on implicit temporal constraints, lacking the capability of handling more complex temporal queries, and struggle with limited reasoning abilities and error propagation in decomposition frameworks. We propose RTQA, a novel framework to address these challenges by enhancing reasoning over TKGs without requiring training. Following recursive thinking, RTQA recursively decomposes questions into sub-problems, solves them bottom-up using LLMs and TKG knowledge, and employs multi-path answer aggregation to improve fault tolerance. RTQA consists of three core components: the Temporal Question Decomposer, the Recursive Solver, and the Answer Aggregator. Experiments on MultiTQ and TimelineKGQA benchmarks demonstrate significant Hits@1 improvements in "Multiple" and "Complex" categories, outperforming state-of-the-art methods. Our code and data are available at https://github.com/zjukg/RTQA.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RTQA: Recursive Thinking for Complex Temporal Knowledge Graph Question Answering with Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RTQA, a novel framework for temporal knowledge graph question answering (TKGQA). RTQA addresses the limitations of existing methods by employing recursive thinking.  It decomposes complex temporal questions into simpler sub-problems, solves them bottom-up using Large Language Models (LLMs) and TKG knowledge, and uses multi-path answer aggregation for improved fault tolerance.  The framework consists of three key components: a Temporal Question Decomposer, a Recursive Solver, and an Answer Aggregator. Experiments on MultiTQ and TimelineKGQA benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements, particularly in "Multiple" and "Complex" question categories, outperforming state-of-the-art methods. The framework is training-free and plug-and-play, enhancing its practicality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The recursive question decomposition is a sound approach for handling complex temporal reasoning. Breaking down intricate questions into manageable sub-problems mirrors human problem-solving strategies and allows LLMs to focus on specific aspects of the query.

*   **Multi-Path Answer Aggregation:**  Addressing the error propagation issue is a crucial contribution. The multi-path answer aggregation module adds robustness by considering multiple answer sources and selecting the most reliable response. This is a significant step toward building more reliable TKGQA systems.

*   **Training-Free and Plug-and-Play:** The framework's design is commendable. The training-free aspect makes it highly accessible, requiring no computational overhead or retraining for different LLMs. The plug-and-play architecture promotes wider adoption and integration with existing systems.

*   **Strong Experimental Results:**  The experimental results on MultiTQ and TimelineKGQA provide compelling evidence of RTQA's effectiveness.  The gains in the "Multiple" and "Complex" question categories demonstrate its ability to handle challenging temporal constraints. The ablation studies clearly showcase the contribution of each component.

*   **Detailed analysis:** The paper provides a through exploration of the datasets used and analyzes the characteristics of T and the API efficiency, making the results better to understand and interpret.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on LLM Quality:** The effectiveness of the question decomposition and recursive solving heavily relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLM.  The paper acknowledges this limitation, stating that smaller models may struggle to generate high-quality sub-questions. This dependency introduces a potential bottleneck and limits the framework's applicability to resource-constrained environments. The framework is limited by the LLM's ability to reason and retrieve information. It won't provide any answer if the LLM cannot solve any of the sub-questions.

*   **Retriever Bottleneck:** RTQA depends on a robust fact retrieval to extract relevant TKG knowledge. If the retrieval process fails to retrieve key information, the LLM may provide inaccurate answers. Although the study of the retrieval process is mentioned in the paper, it seems not that in-depth and deserves a further investigation.

*   **Limited Generalizability Claims:** While the experiments cover two challenging TKGQA benchmarks, the paper acknowledges that the framework is primarily tailored for complex *temporal* questions.  Its applicability to other QA domains or general question-answering tasks is not thoroughly validated.

*   **API-bound Experiments**: All experiments were performed by utilizing the OpenAI and Deepseek APIs, making the framework limited by the stability and performance of the 3rd party APIs.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's main novelty lies in the recursive question decomposition approach and the multi-path answer aggregation strategy. While question decomposition is not entirely new in KGQA, the application to *temporal* knowledge graphs, coupled with the error mitigation strategy, represents a significant advancement. The plug-and-play architecture and training-free nature also contribute to its practical value. The significant gains demonstrated in complex temporal queries address a recognized gap in existing TKGQA methods. The framework provides a strong foundation for future research on enhancing the robustness and generalizability of TKGQA systems. The contributions of the research have a meaningful influence on the TKGQA and the broader research community.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to a relevant and challenging problem in TKGQA. The strengths in design and demonstrated performance justify a positive evaluation. However, the reliance on LLM capabilities, retriever quality, and API limitations restrict the paper from achieving a higher score. These dependencies highlight areas for future work to enhance the framework's robustness and broader applicability. The limited experiments bound to OpenAI and Deepseek API and not comparing between them also detracts from the score.

In summary, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field, but has room to grow.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AutoPBO: LLM-powered Optimization for Local Search PBO Solvers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04007v1)
- **Authors**: Jinyuan Li, Yi Chu, Yiwen Sun, Mengchuan Zou, Shaowei Cai
- **Abstract**: Pseudo-Boolean Optimization (PBO) provides a powerful framework for modeling combinatorial problems through pseudo-Boolean (PB) constraints. Local search solvers have shown excellent performance in PBO solving, and their efficiency is highly dependent on their internal heuristics to guide the search. Still, their design often requires significant expert effort and manual tuning in practice. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated potential in automating algorithm design, their application to optimizing PBO solvers remains unexplored. In this work, we introduce AutoPBO, a novel LLM-powered framework to automatically enhance PBO local search solvers. We conduct experiments on a broad range of four public benchmarks, including one real-world benchmark, a benchmark from PB competition, an integer linear programming optimization benchmark, and a crafted combinatorial benchmark, to evaluate the performance improvement achieved by AutoPBO and compare it with six state-of-the-art competitors, including two local search PBO solvers NuPBO and OraSLS, two complete PB solvers PBO-IHS and RoundingSat, and two mixed integer programming (MIP) solvers Gurobi and SCIP. AutoPBO demonstrates significant improvements over previous local search approaches, while maintaining competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art competitors. The results suggest that AutoPBO offers a promising approach to automating local search solver design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoPBO, a novel framework that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically enhance local search solvers for Pseudo-Boolean Optimization (PBO) problems. AutoPBO uses a multi-agent system with a greedy search strategy to enable closed-loop, feedback-driven optimization of the solvers. The framework incorporates a structuralized local search PBO solver (StructPBO) with a clearer code structure as input for LLMs. The paper evaluates AutoPBO on a range of benchmark datasets against state-of-the-art solvers, including complete solvers and Mixed Integer Programming (MIP) solvers.  The experimental results suggest that AutoPBO improves upon existing local search approaches while remaining competitive with established solvers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:** The paper presents a novel approach by applying LLMs to automatically enhance the heuristics of local search PBO solvers. This is a relatively unexplored area, especially in the context of PBO. The proposed multi-agent system and the structuralized PBO solver are well-designed components.
*   **Practical Significance:** PBO is a widely used framework for modeling combinatorial problems, and improving the efficiency of PBO solvers has practical implications in various domains.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are conducted on a broad range of benchmark datasets, including real-world, PB competition, integer linear programming, and combinatorial benchmarks. The comparison with several state-of-the-art solvers provides a solid basis for evaluating AutoPBO's performance.
*   **Consistent Improvements:**  The experimental results show that AutoPBO consistently improves the performance of the baseline solver (StructPBO) across multiple benchmarks.
*   **Clear and Well-Structured:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and easy to understand. The methodology is well-explained.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on DeepSeek LLM:** The framework relies on a specific LLM (DeepSeek) and it's performance may vary for other LLMs. The paper does not provide an analysis of the LLM's impact on the overall performance or a comparison of different LLMs.
*   **Incremental Improvement over SOTA:** While AutoPBO demonstrates improvements over the baseline, the degree of improvement compared to commercial solvers like Gurobi is not always significant. There may be cases where AutoPBO struggles to outperform highly optimized commercial tools.
*   **Generalizability Concerns:** While tests were conducted on various benchmarks, it is unclear how well the optimized solvers generated by AutoPBO would generalize to significantly different or new PBO problem instances.
*   **Explainability:** Like many LLM-based approaches, it may be difficult to understand why certain heuristics were generated and why they work. This lack of explainability can be a disadvantage in some applications.
*   **Limited Novelty in StructPBO:** While the paper highlights StructPBO as a structured framework to better fit the LLM, the novelty of this framework is limited. Also, it doesn't seem to provide significant benefits compared to prior art, based on the relatively limited improvement in perfromance.
*   **Lack of Analysis of Generated Code:** The paper doesn't provide detailed analysis of the code actually generated by LLMs. Is it truly novel, or just subtle parameter variations? This is essential for determining the true algorithmic contribution.

**Justification of Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7** to this paper. While the application of LLMs to automatically improve PBO solvers is a novel and promising direction, the limited quantitative gains over established solvers, and the dependence on a specific LLM temper its overall significance. The thorough experimentation and clear presentation are definite strengths. However, the weaknesses related to the depth of LLM-generated code analysis, the generalizability concerns, the limited novelty in the structured solver and explainability concerns prevent a higher score. There is definite potential for future work to build on these ideas.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Detecting Regional Spurious Correlations in Vision Transformers via Token Discarding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04009v1)
- **Authors**: Solha Kang, Esla Timothy Anzaku, Wesley De Neve, Arnout Van Messem, Joris Vankerschaver, Francois Rameau, Utku Ozbulak
- **Abstract**: Due to their powerful feature association capabilities, neural network-based computer vision models have the ability to detect and exploit unintended patterns within the data, potentially leading to correct predictions based on incorrect or unintended but statistically relevant signals. These clues may vary from simple color aberrations to small texts within the image. In situations where these unintended signals align with the predictive task, models can mistakenly link these features with the task and rely on them for making predictions. This phenomenon is referred to as spurious correlations, where patterns appear to be associated with the task but are actually coincidental. As a result, detection and mitigation of spurious correlations have become crucial tasks for building trustworthy, reliable, and generalizable machine learning models. In this work, we present a novel method to detect spurious correlations in vision transformers, a type of neural network architecture that gained significant popularity in recent years. Using both supervised and self-supervised trained models, we present large-scale experiments on the ImageNet dataset demonstrating the ability of the proposed method to identify spurious correlations. We also find that, even if the same architecture is used, the training methodology has a significant impact on the model's reliance on spurious correlations. Furthermore, we show that certain classes in the ImageNet dataset contain spurious signals that are easily detected by the models and discuss the underlying reasons for those spurious signals. In light of our findings, we provide an exhaustive list of the aforementioned images and call for caution in their use in future research efforts. Lastly, we present a case study investigating spurious signals in invasive breast mass classification, grounding our work in real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for detecting regional spurious correlations in Vision Transformers (ViTs) using a token discarding mechanism.  The core idea is to identify influential tokens (those that significantly impact the model's prediction) and then assess whether these tokens are located within the object's bounding box (core features) or outside (potentially spurious features). The paper proposes two metrics, the Average Token Spuriosity Index (A-TSI) and the Maximum Token Spuriosity Index (M-TSI), to quantify the extent of spurious correlations. Through experiments on ImageNet, the authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their method, showing that training methodology significantly impacts a model's reliance on spurious correlations and identifying problematic classes in ImageNet that are easily classified based on non-core features. A case study on breast mass classification is also presented to ground the work in a real-world scenario.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the specific application of token discarding to *detect* rather than prevent spurious correlations in ViTs.  While token discarding itself isn't new, using it to identify and quantify the *location* of influential tokens relative to ground truth bounding boxes to assess spuriosity is a clear contribution.  The two metrics (A-TSI and M-TSI) are also novel in their specific formulation to capture average and maximum spuriosity. There's also some innovation in identifying specific classes in ImageNet that are prone to such correlations.

*   **Significance:** The significance is moderate.  Identifying and mitigating spurious correlations is a critical challenge for building trustworthy and generalizable ML models.  The paper offers a new tool for *analyzing* trained models, which is valuable. The ability to identify classes with systematic issues within datasets like ImageNet is also significant. The case study helps showcase the practicality of the approach in a medical imaging domain. However, the method primarily focuses on *detection*, not *mitigation*, limiting its direct impact on improving model performance in the face of spurious correlations.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined method.
    *   Extensive experiments on a large dataset (ImageNet).
    *   Analysis of the impact of different training methodologies (supervised vs. self-supervised).
    *   Identification of problematic classes in ImageNet and discussion of underlying reasons.
    *   Real-world case study.
    *   The connection to missingness bias and how ViTs naturally mitigate it is a good point.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Computational cost: Token discarding is computationally expensive, limiting scalability.
    *   Reliance on annotations: Requires bounding box annotations, which may not always be available. The paper addresses this to some extent by exploring the use of attention maps as a proxy for bounding boxes, but the results are mixed.
    *   Limited direct impact on *mitigation*: The method primarily focuses on *detecting* spurious correlations, not directly addressing how to prevent or remove them.
    *   The dependence on a well-trained model: The analysis relies on the pre-trained model's ability to classify, which could be a limitation.
    *  While A-TSI and M-TSI have clear definitions, more investigation of how to best combine the metrics would be useful.
    * The case study on medical images, although provides a real-world application, needs more evaluation and analysis to highlight the significant role of the purposed method in breast cancer detection.
    * Some of the findings regarding ImageNet classes that are highly vulnerable to spurious features are previously discovered in exisiting works which weakens the contribution of the work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence will likely be in providing a new analysis tool for researchers working on ViTs and spurious correlations.  It could also influence dataset curation efforts by highlighting problematic classes and annotation issues in ImageNet.  The method could be used as a diagnostic tool to assess the robustness of ViTs trained on different datasets or using different training techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novel application of token discarding and the detailed analysis, the paper provides a useful method for the field.  The limitations regarding computational cost and annotation reliance, combined with the limited focus on *mitigation*, prevent a higher score.  The significance is moderate, and while the paper provides a novel perspective, its direct impact on improving model performance is limited. Therefore, a score of 6 reflects the novelty and value of the analysis while acknowledging the limitations and scope of the contribution.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### NER Retriever: Zero-Shot Named Entity Retrieval with Type-Aware Embeddings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04011v1)
- **Authors**: Or Shachar, Uri Katz, Yoav Goldberg, Oren Glickman
- **Abstract**: We present NER Retriever, a zero-shot retrieval framework for ad-hoc Named Entity Retrieval, a variant of Named Entity Recognition (NER), where the types of interest are not provided in advance, and a user-defined type description is used to retrieve documents mentioning entities of that type. Instead of relying on fixed schemas or fine-tuned models, our method builds on internal representations of large language models (LLMs) to embed both entity mentions and user-provided open-ended type descriptions into a shared semantic space. We show that internal representations, specifically the value vectors from mid-layer transformer blocks, encode fine-grained type information more effectively than commonly used top-layer embeddings. To refine these representations, we train a lightweight contrastive projection network that aligns type-compatible entities while separating unrelated types. The resulting entity embeddings are compact, type-aware, and well-suited for nearest-neighbor search. Evaluated on three benchmarks, NER Retriever significantly outperforms both lexical and dense sentence-level retrieval baselines. Our findings provide empirical support for representation selection within LLMs and demonstrate a practical solution for scalable, schema-free entity retrieval. The NER Retriever Codebase is publicly available at https://github.com/ShacharOr100/ner_retriever
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces NER Retriever, a novel zero-shot named entity retrieval framework.  NER Retriever aims to address the challenge of ad-hoc named entity recognition, where the entity types of interest are not predefined. The core idea is to leverage internal representations of large language models (LLMs) to embed both entity mentions and user-provided type descriptions into a shared semantic space. The authors discover that mid-layer transformer blocks within LLMs are more effective at encoding fine-grained type information than top-layer embeddings. They refine these representations using a contrastive projection network that aligns type-compatible entities while separating unrelated types, making them suitable for nearest-neighbor search. The system is evaluated on three benchmarks, demonstrating superior performance compared to lexical and dense sentence-level retrieval baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates a clever application of LLMs for zero-shot named entity retrieval, a task with increasing practical relevance. While the concept of using LLMs for zero-shot NER isn't entirely new, the paper's key innovations lie in:

    *   **Mid-Layer Representation Discovery:** The authors present strong empirical evidence that intermediate layers within LLMs encode richer type information compared to final layers. This is a valuable and non-trivial finding with implications beyond this specific task.
    *   **Contrastive Projection Network:**  Using a lightweight contrastive projection network to refine the raw LLM embeddings is a smart approach for creating a type-aware, low-dimensional embedding space suitable for efficient retrieval.
    *   **Practicality:** The NER Retriever framework is presented as a practical solution for scalable, schema-free entity retrieval, supported by a publicly available codebase.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of information retrieval and named entity recognition. It provides a practical solution for retrieving entities of ad-hoc types, addressing a limitation of traditional NER systems that rely on fixed schemas. The empirical findings regarding the effectiveness of mid-layer LLM representations contribute to our understanding of how knowledge is encoded within these models. The results, particularly the gains on the challenging MultiCoNER 2 dataset, are compelling.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-motivated problem and clear presentation.
    *   Strong empirical results across multiple benchmarks.
    *   Detailed ablation studies that provide insights into the system's design choices.
    *   Release of the code for reproducibility and further research.
    *   Analysis that goes beyond simply reporting performance, but also investigates *why* certain design choices are effective.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on an external entity span detector, though practical, introduces a potential source of error that could limit the overall performance. The ablation study with the "entity span oracle" highlights this limitation, but more detailed analysis of error propagation from the span detector would be valuable.
    *   The comparative evaluation could be strengthened by including more competitive zero-shot NER baselines, if available. While the baselines used are standard for *retrieval*, are there zero-shot NER methods that are directly comparable?
    *   The evaluation is limited to R-Precision. Including other standard retrieval metrics like MAP or nDCG would provide a more comprehensive view of the system's performance.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper's findings are likely to encourage further research into exploring intermediate layers of LLMs for various NLP tasks.
    *   The NER Retriever framework could be adopted as a practical solution for ad-hoc entity retrieval in various applications.
    *   The contrastive learning approach for type-aware entity embeddings could be extended and applied to other tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to zero-shot named entity retrieval, backed by strong empirical evidence and insightful analysis. The key innovation lies in identifying and exploiting the type-encoding capabilities of mid-layer LLM representations, combined with a lightweight contrastive learning approach. While there are minor weaknesses in the evaluation and reliance on an external component, the paper's strengths outweigh its limitations. It significantly advances the state-of-the-art in ad-hoc entity retrieval and provides valuable insights into the internal workings of LLMs. It provides both a practical system and a novel research direction, which contribute to the field. Therefore, a score of 8 is justified.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### On Robustness and Reliability of Benchmark-Based Evaluation of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04013v1)
- **Authors**: Riccardo Lunardi, Vincenzo Della Mea, Stefano Mizzaro, Kevin Roitero
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) effectiveness is usually evaluated by means of benchmarks such as MMLU, ARC-C, or HellaSwag, where questions are presented in their original wording, thus in a fixed, standardized format. However, real-world applications involve linguistic variability, requiring models to maintain their effectiveness across diverse rewordings of the same question or query. In this study, we systematically assess the robustness of LLMs to paraphrased benchmark questions and investigate whether benchmark-based evaluations provide a reliable measure of model capabilities. We systematically generate various paraphrases of all the questions across six different common benchmarks, and measure the resulting variations in effectiveness of 34 state-of-the-art LLMs, of different size and effectiveness. Our findings reveal that while LLM rankings remain relatively stable across paraphrased inputs, absolute effectiveness scores change, and decline significantly. This suggests that LLMs struggle with linguistic variability, raising concerns about their generalization abilities and evaluation methodologies. Furthermore, the observed performance drop challenges the reliability of benchmark-based evaluations, indicating that high benchmark scores may not fully capture a model's robustness to real-world input variations. We discuss the implications of these findings for LLM evaluation methodologies, emphasizing the need for robustness-aware benchmarks that better reflect practical deployment scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "On Robustness and Reliability of Benchmark-Based Evaluation of LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness and reliability of standard benchmark evaluations for Large Language Models (LLMs). The authors systematically paraphrase questions from six common benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-C, HellaSwag, OpenBookQA, RACE, SciQ) and evaluate the performance of 34 state-of-the-art LLMs on both the original and paraphrased questions. Their findings show that while the relative ranking of models tends to remain stable, absolute performance (accuracy) significantly decreases when models are presented with paraphrased questions. This suggests that current benchmark evaluations might overestimate the true generalization capabilities and robustness of LLMs to linguistic variations. The paper also explores the relationship between model consistency (providing the same answer across paraphrases) and accuracy, revealing that less capable models can exhibit high consistency but be frequently wrong, while more advanced models achieve high consistency along with higher accuracy. Finally, the study finds evidence suggesting potential data contamination in older benchmarks, where models perform better on the original questions than on paraphrased versions, possibly due to overfitting or memorization effects.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's core idea of systematically assessing LLM robustness via paraphrasing benchmark questions has merit, but the originality is somewhat limited. While previous work has investigated prompt variations and the reliability of benchmarks, this paper is among the first to focus explicitly on *question* paraphrasing across several popular benchmarks and a wide range of LLMs in a multiple-choice setting.  The analysis of model consistency as a metric and its relationship to accuracy does add some novelty. The finding regarding potential data contamination, although not entirely novel in the broader context of LLM research, adds value in the specific context of these benchmarks.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a critical issue: whether current benchmarks provide a reliable and robust measure of LLM capabilities. The findings have important implications for how we evaluate and interpret benchmark scores. If models perform significantly worse on paraphrased questions, it suggests they are not truly understanding the underlying concepts but rather relying on surface-level patterns present in the training data or the specific benchmark formulation. This calls for a re-evaluation of existing benchmarks and the development of more robust evaluation methodologies. The finding regarding data contamination also raises concerns about the validity of benchmark results and the need for careful curation of benchmark datasets.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Approach:** The authors employ a systematic and well-defined methodology, paraphrasing questions from multiple benchmarks and evaluating a large number of LLMs.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of the results, exploring the impact of paraphrasing on model accuracy, consistency, and rankings.
*   **Important Insights:** The findings highlight the limitations of current benchmark evaluations and the need for more robust evaluation methodologies.
*   **Open Data:**  The availability of data and code is excellent, fostering reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Automatic Paraphrasing Limitations:** While the use of automatic paraphrasing is practical for scaling the study, it might not capture the full range of linguistic variations found in real-world applications.  Manual paraphrasing or the inclusion of user-generated paraphrases could strengthen the validity of the findings.
*   **Limited Exploration of Reasoning Strategies:** The paper primarily focuses on zero-shot evaluation. The exploration of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting is limited and lacks a deep dive into how paraphrasing affects reasoning abilities. Including more complex reasoning strategies is important for future research.
*   **Generalizability of Findings:**  While the six benchmarks are common, the results may not generalize to all types of LLM tasks or benchmarks (e.g., open-ended generation tasks).
*   **Data Contamination is a Conjecture:** The study suggests potential data contamination but does not provide definitive proof.  A more thorough investigation would involve analyzing the training datasets of the models. The study is limited to making inferences based on trends in accuracy.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by systematically investigating the robustness of LLMs to question paraphrasing. The findings are relevant and have implications for how we interpret benchmark scores and design future evaluation methodologies. However, the limitations related to automatic paraphrasing, the shallow exploration of reasoning strategies, and the conjectural nature of the data contamination analysis somewhat limit the paper's overall impact.  Therefore:

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CoT-Space: A Theoretical Framework for Internal Slow-Thinking via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04027v1)
- **Authors**: Zeyu Gan, Hao Yi, Yong Liu
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has become a pivotal approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, a significant theoretical gap persists, as traditional token-level RL frameworks fail to align with the reasoning-level nature of complex, multi-step thought processes like Chain-of-Thought (CoT). To address this challenge, we introduce CoT-Space, a novel theoretical framework that recasts LLM reasoning from a discrete token-prediction task to an optimization process within a continuous, reasoning-level semantic space. By analyzing this process from both a noise perspective and a risk perspective, we demonstrate that the convergence to an optimal CoT length is a natural consequence of the fundamental trade-off between underfitting and overfitting. Furthermore, extensive experiments provide strong empirical validation for our theoretical findings. Our framework not only provides a coherent explanation for empirical phenomena such as overthinking but also offers a solid theoretical foundation to guide the future development of more effective and generalizable reasoning agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CoT-Space: A Theoretical Framework for Internal Slow-Thinking via Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CoT-Space, a novel theoretical framework for analyzing the reasoning process of Large Language Models (LLMs) when enhanced with Reinforcement Learning (RL).  Instead of the traditional token-level RL view, CoT-Space recasts the reasoning process as an optimization problem within a continuous, reasoning-level semantic space.  The paper analyzes this process from both a noise and a risk perspective, demonstrating that convergence to an optimal Chain-of-Thought (CoT) length is a natural consequence of balancing underfitting and overfitting.  The paper provides empirical validation for its theoretical findings, offering an explanation for the "overthinking" phenomenon and a foundation for developing more effective reasoning agents. The core of the paper leverages a shift from discrete, token-level analysis to a continuous, reasoning-level perspective, drawing an analogy to optimization and noise in machine learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its theoretical framework, CoT-Space, which provides a high-level abstraction for LLM reasoning. The shift from a token-level Markov Decision Process (MDP) to a continuous optimization process in a semantic space is a significant conceptual leap. The analogy drawn to sharpness-aware minimization in classical ML is insightful. However, the individual components of the analysis (PAC-Bayes bounds, bias-variance tradeoff) are well-established in learning theory. The novelty primarily comes from *how* these concepts are applied to LLM reasoning. The claims of providing novel analysis for two established phenomena are ambitious: the novelty of the overthinking and generalization analysis stems from the perspective provided by the framework, and whether this perspective offers insights beyond the existing empirical knowledge about these phenomena is questionable.

**Significance:** The significance of this work hinges on its ability to provide a more principled understanding of LLM reasoning and guide future development. The CoT-Space framework could potentially enable the design of more efficient RL algorithms for training LLMs and improve their reasoning capabilities. The analysis of noise and risk provides a theoretical basis for regularization techniques. One weakness is that the theoretical claims are validated through a very specific set of experimental setups with mathematical reasoning as the use case, and the experimental results don't showcase compelling empirical advantages in terms of the key LLM benchmarks. In addition, the complexity in the formalism makes the approach hard to follow and understand.

**Strengths:**

*   **Theoretical Rigor:** The paper is well-grounded in established concepts from learning theory and optimization.
*   **Conceptual Framework:** CoT-Space offers a novel and potentially useful way to think about LLM reasoning.
*   **Explanation of Overthinking:** The paper provides a plausible theoretical explanation for the empirical observation of the overthinking phenomenon.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments, while targeted, offer some support for the theoretical claims.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** The mathematical formalism can be dense and difficult to follow, potentially limiting its accessibility.
*   **Limited Generalizability:**  The experiments primarily focus on mathematical reasoning; the framework's applicability to other reasoning tasks needs further investigation.
*   **Incremental Insights:** Some analyses use complex machinery for well-understood phenomena, and the amount of actionable insights gained is relatively incremental.
*   **Practical Impact:** The direct practical implications of the CoT-Space framework are not yet clear. The paper doesn't demonstrate how this framework leads to substantially improved RL algorithms or LLM performance.
*   **Limited novelty in the analyses**: Existing theories of generalization and the bias-variance tradeoff are adapted to the specific context of LLM reasoning. This adaptation is insightful, but the resulting claims are not entirely novel.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper provides a conceptually novel framework that reinterprets LLM reasoning in a way that leverages concepts from theoretical machine learning to explain known phenomena. However, the framework's complexity, limited empirical validation, and incremental insights prevent it from having an immediate transformative impact. The value rests more on its potential as a guiding principle and its ability to connect existing empirical understanding to established theory. The theoretical elegance of the framework is countered by the practical challenge of translating these ideas into directly improved algorithms or demonstrable performance gains across the broad spectrum of reasoning tasks. For these reasons, a score in the lower half of the distribution seems appropriate.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### SMooGPT: Stylized Motion Generation using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04058v1)
- **Authors**: Lei Zhong, Yi Yang, Changjian Li
- **Abstract**: Stylized motion generation is actively studied in computer graphics, especially benefiting from the rapid advances in diffusion models. The goal of this task is to produce a novel motion respecting both the motion content and the desired motion style, e.g., ``walking in a loop like a Monkey''. Existing research attempts to address this problem via motion style transfer or conditional motion generation. They typically embed the motion style into a latent space and guide the motion implicitly in a latent space as well. Despite the progress, their methods suffer from low interpretability and control, limited generalization to new styles, and fail to produce motions other than ``walking'' due to the strong bias in the public stylization dataset. In this paper, we propose to solve the stylized motion generation problem from a new perspective of reasoning-composition-generation, based on our observations: i) human motion can often be effectively described using natural language in a body-part centric manner, ii) LLMs exhibit a strong ability to understand and reason about human motion, and iii) human motion has an inherently compositional nature, facilitating the new motion content or style generation via effective recomposing. We thus propose utilizing body-part text space as an intermediate representation, and present SMooGPT, a fine-tuned LLM, acting as a reasoner, composer, and generator when generating the desired stylized motion. Our method executes in the body-part text space with much higher interpretability, enabling fine-grained motion control, effectively resolving potential conflicts between motion content and style, and generalizes well to new styles thanks to the open-vocabulary ability of LLMs. Comprehensive experiments and evaluations, and a user perceptual study, demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, especially under the pure text-driven stylized motion generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SMooGPT, a novel approach for stylized motion generation that leverages large language models (LLMs).  Unlike previous methods that embed motion styles into a latent space, SMooGPT operates in a "body-part text space" as an intermediate representation. This involves a three-stage process: (1) *Reasoning*: The LLM translates motion content and desired style (given as text or motion sequences) into text descriptions of individual body part movements. (2) *Composition*: The LLM combines the body-part texts for content and style, resolving potential conflicts.  (3) *Generation*: The LLM generates the final stylized motion from the composed body-part texts. The paper fine-tunes a pre-trained LLM with curated datasets to enable this process, achieving state-of-the-art results, particularly in pure text-driven stylization, and demonstrating improved generalization to new styles. The key idea is that human motion, style, and language are intrinsically linked, and LLMs can effectively reason about and compose them to generate controllable and interpretable stylized motions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel perspective on stylized motion generation by framing it as a reasoning-composition-generation problem within the body-part text space. The core innovation lies in utilizing LLMs not just as generators but as reasoners and composers of motion descriptions. This is a significant departure from traditional style transfer and conditional generation methods, which often rely on latent space manipulations and may lack interpretability and control. The use of body-part centric descriptions is also a solid choice given its inherent advantages in allowing for fine-grained compositionality.

**Significance:** The paper addresses key limitations of existing methods, such as low interpretability, limited control, and poor generalization to new styles. By leveraging the reasoning capabilities of LLMs, the proposed approach achieves better control over motion, effectively resolves conflicts between content and style, and generalizes well to unseen styles. The demonstrated ability to generate diverse and realistic stylized motions based on pure text inputs has a significant impact. It opens up new possibilities for applications such as character animation, virtual reality, and motion design.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty and Conceptual Soundness:**  The core idea of using LLMs to reason about motion styles and compose body-part descriptions is innovative and well-justified.
*   **Improved Control and Interpretability:** The body-part text space allows for fine-grained control over motion and provides a more interpretable representation of stylized motion.
*   **Generalization:** The open-vocabulary nature of LLMs allows for better generalization to new styles compared to methods that rely on pre-defined style datasets.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** Comprehensive experiments and user studies demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The approach consistently outperforms baselines, particularly in text-guided stylization.
*   **Versatility:** The method can generate motion from text or motion inputs for both content and style, which is nice from an application perspective.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** The performance of SMooGPT heavily relies on the capabilities of the underlying LLM. Any limitations of the LLM, such as biases or inability to capture nuanced aspects of human motion, could affect the generated results.
*   **Complexity:**  The system involves several components, including the VQ-VAEs and the fine-tuned LLM, which might increase the complexity. A more thorough discussion and ablation study of these design choices could benefit the paper.
*   **Computational Cost:** The current reliance on LLMs might pose some computational challenges. While inference time is provided, a comparison with competing methods would be valuable.
*   **Limited Dynamics in Body-Part Space:** The discussion mentions limitations in the expressiveness of the body-part text space for representing intricate dynamics, but it would be good to see this explored in more detail. While the suggestion of keypose-based descriptions addresses this, the paper doesn't validate this approach.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of motion generation. The use of LLMs as reasoners and composers for motion opens a new avenue for research. The body-part text space provides a valuable framework for motion control and interpretability.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel, significant, and well-executed approach to stylized motion generation. The idea of using LLMs for reasoning and composition within the body-part text space is very compelling. While the paper acknowledges some limitations, the results are strong, the experimental validation is thorough, and the potential impact on the field is high. The weaknesses mentioned above are minor and do not significantly detract from the overall contribution. The main weakness is the reliance on LLMs, which, while powerful, can have limitations in expressiveness.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Synthesizing Sheet Music Problems for Evaluation and Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04059v1)
- **Authors**: Zhilin Wang, Zhe Yang, Yun Luo, Yafu Li, Haoran Zhang, Runzhe Zhan, Derek F. Wong, Jizhe Zhou, Yu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Enhancing the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to interpret sheet music is a crucial step toward building AI musicians. However, current research lacks both evaluation benchmarks and training data for sheet music reasoning. To address this, we propose the idea of synthesizing sheet music problems grounded in music theory, which can serve both as evaluation benchmarks and as training data for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). We introduce a data synthesis framework that generates verifiable sheet music questions in both textual and visual modalities, leading to the Synthetic Sheet Music Reasoning Benchmark (SSMR-Bench) and a complementary training set. Evaluation results on SSMR-Bench show the importance of models' reasoning abilities in interpreting sheet music. At the same time, the poor performance of Gemini 2.5-Pro highlights the challenges that MLLMs still face in interpreting sheet music in a visual format. By leveraging synthetic data for RLVR, Qwen3-8B-Base and Qwen2.5-VL-Instruct achieve improvements on the SSMR-Bench. Besides, the trained Qwen3-8B-Base surpasses GPT-4 in overall performance on MusicTheoryBench and achieves reasoning performance comparable to GPT-4 with the strategies of Role play and Chain-of-Thought. Notably, its performance on math problems also improves relative to the original Qwen3-8B-Base. Furthermore, our results show that the enhanced reasoning ability can also facilitate music composition. In conclusion, we are the first to propose the idea of synthesizing sheet music problems based on music theory rules, and demonstrate its effectiveness not only in advancing model reasoning for sheet music understanding but also in unlocking new possibilities for AI-assisted music creation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of benchmarks and training data for sheet music reasoning in LLMs and MLLMs. It proposes a novel approach: synthesizing sheet music problems based on music theory rules. This allows for both evaluation (via a new benchmark, SSMR-Bench) and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). The authors develop a data synthesis framework that generates textual and visual sheet music questions, train models using this synthetic data, and demonstrate improved performance on sheet music understanding and even preliminary music composition tasks.  They show that MLLMs struggle with visual sheet music compared to textual representations and that RLVR with synthetic data improves performance on both modalities.  Furthermore, training on their synthetic data improves performance on the MusicTheoryBench benchmark and even improves math reasoning ability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of synthesizing sheet music problems based on music theory rules for both benchmarking and RLVR is indeed novel. While data synthesis has been explored in other domains, its application to sheet music reasoning is a unique contribution. The creation of the SSMR-Bench is also a valuable addition, providing a standardized way to evaluate models on this task. While other benchmarks exists, the approach to make this one and a data source is fully programmatic and scalable.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to unlock new possibilities in AI-assisted music creation. By improving the ability of LLMs and MLLMs to interpret sheet music, the paper contributes to building AI musicians. The RLVR approach with verifiable rewards offers a promising avenue for training models with strong reasoning abilities in a verifiable way, which could be expanded upon in different contexts beyond music. The improvement in both musical and mathematical domains also indicates the potential for transferable skills learned with the proposed approach.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies a gap in the literature regarding sheet music reasoning.
    *   **Creative solution:** The proposed data synthesis framework is a clever way to address the lack of real-world data and enables RLVR in this field.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experiments are thorough, evaluating the framework on both a newly introduced benchmark and existing ones.
    *   **Demonstrated improvements:** The results clearly show that training on synthetic data improves model performance on various tasks, suggesting the effectiveness of the approach.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The tool can be used to help train more capable agents for music-related tasks.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simplicity of questions:** The paper itself acknowledges that the generated questions are relatively simple and can be reliably solved by a strong pre-trained language model. While this simplifies verification, it also limits the complexity of reasoning tasks that can be evaluated.
    *   **Limited composition results:** While the results on music composition are encouraging, the evaluation is focused solely on rhythmic consistency, which is just one aspect of music composition. A more holistic evaluation of musicality and creativity would strengthen this claim.
    *   **Focus on a specific music notation format:** The paper focuses heavily on ABC notation. While useful for programmatic manipulation, its limited adoption compared to visual staff notation could hinder the practical impact of this work.
    *   **Limited investigation into transfer learning:** The positive result related to math is promising but is not followed up by further experimentation. More investigation is warranted into the cause for this transfer learning.

**Score and Justification:**

Considering the novelty and significance weighed against the weaknesses, I would assign this paper a score of **7**.

*   **Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to address a critical gap in the field of AI-assisted music creation. The data synthesis framework and SSMR-Bench are valuable contributions, and the results clearly demonstrate improved model performance. However, the simplicity of the generated questions, the limited evaluation of music composition, and the focus on a specific notation format suggest that there is significant room for future work to further enhance and expand upon this approach. Still, given that the authors are the first to introduce this concept, and has a large impact from the presented results, this is certainly a solid paper.

### Arabic Chatbot Technologies in Education: An Overview
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04066v1)
- **Authors**: Hicham Bourhil, Yacine El Younoussi
- **Abstract**: The recent advancements in Artificial Intelligence (AI) in general, and in Natural Language Processing (NLP) in particular, and some of its applications such as chatbots, have led to their implementation in different domains like education, healthcare, tourism, and customer service. Since the COVID-19 pandemic, there has been an increasing interest in these digital technologies to allow and enhance remote access. In education, e-learning systems have been massively adopted worldwide. The emergence of Large Language Models (LLM) such as BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) and GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformers) made chatbots even more popular. In this study, we present a survey on existing Arabic chatbots in education and their different characteristics such as the adopted approaches, language variety, and metrics used to measure their performance. We were able to identified some research gaps when we discovered that, despite the success of chatbots in other languages such as English, only a few educational Arabic chatbots used modern techniques. Finally, we discuss future directions of research in this field.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a survey of Arabic chatbot technologies in education. It highlights the increasing interest in chatbots, particularly post-COVID-19, for enhancing remote learning.  The authors note the relative immaturity of Arabic chatbots compared to English ones, citing limited use of advanced AI techniques (generative models) and a reliance on retrieval-based approaches and MSA. The survey identifies a small number of unique educational Arabic chatbots and discusses their characteristics, including language variety, adopted approaches, and evaluation metrics.  It identifies a gap in supporting Classical Arabic and dialects and calls for more extensive Arabic datasets. It concludes that automated, standardized evaluation metrics are needed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper claims to be the first survey focusing specifically on *educational* Arabic chatbots. While this niche focus lends some novelty, the survey is based on two existing reviews from 2022, supplemented by more recent studies. The core argument about the immaturity of Arabic chatbots in education is not fundamentally new. Other surveys have pointed to this, but perhaps not with this specific focus.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in consolidating the existing literature and specifically highlighting the shortcomings of Arabic chatbots in the educational domain. The call for increased support for Classical Arabic and dialects is particularly relevant, as it addresses a crucial aspect of linguistic diversity and cultural heritage. The emphasis on automated, standardized evaluation is also important for fostering rigorous development and comparison of chatbots.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined scope: The paper's focus on educational applications provides a clear boundary for the survey.
    *   Identifies key challenges: The paper effectively highlights issues such as the lack of generative models, limited dialect support, and subjective evaluation metrics.
    *   Provides a structured overview: The summary of chatbots and their characteristics is useful for researchers entering the field.
    *   Clear recommendations: The paper concludes with actionable suggestions for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited scope of "new" research: The survey adds only two more bots and recent publications to existing work; the added value of this "new" discovery seems small.

    *   Methodological rigor: The paper offers no new quantitative or qualitative methods; it simply compiles existing reviews and research.
    *   Depth of analysis: While the paper identifies challenges, it doesn't delve deeply into the reasons behind them (e.g., specific challenges of dialect data collection or limitations of available Arabic NLP resources).
    *   Limited Impact: The paper itself is a small contribution to the field, and its impact is largely speculative.

*   **Overall Assessment:**

The paper offers a valuable overview of Arabic chatbots in education, but its incremental novelty and limited scope impact its overall significance. It effectively synthesizes existing research and identifies key areas for improvement, but it doesn't present groundbreaking insights or introduce new methodological approaches. The claim of being the first survey focusing solely on *educational* Arabic chatbots warrants the consideration of uniqueness in its contribution.

**Score: 5/10**

**Justification:** The paper provides a useful, albeit incremental, contribution to the field by consolidating existing literature and highlighting specific challenges in the development of educational Arabic chatbots. However, its novelty is limited by its reliance on existing reviews and the lack of new, in-depth analysis. While the paper offers valuable recommendations, its overall impact is likely to be moderate. The study is useful to newcomers to the field but does not offer groundbreaking information. Therefore, a score of 5 reflects the paper's contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### RepoDebug: Repository-Level Multi-Task and Multi-Language Debugging Evaluation of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04078v1)
- **Authors**: Jingjing Liu, Zeming Liu, Zihao Cheng, Mengliang He, Xiaoming Shi, Yuhang Guo, Xiangrong Zhu, Yuanfang Guo, Yunhong Wang, Haifeng Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have exhibited significant proficiency in code debugging, especially in automatic program repair, which may substantially reduce the time consumption of developers and enhance their efficiency. Significant advancements in debugging datasets have been made to promote the development of code debugging. However, these datasets primarily focus on assessing the LLM's function-level code repair capabilities, neglecting the more complex and realistic repository-level scenarios, which leads to an incomplete understanding of the LLM's challenges in repository-level debugging. While several repository-level datasets have been proposed, they often suffer from limitations such as limited diversity of tasks, languages, and error types. To mitigate this challenge, this paper introduces RepoDebug, a multi-task and multi-language repository-level code debugging dataset with 22 subtypes of errors that supports 8 commonly used programming languages and 3 debugging tasks. Furthermore, we conduct evaluation experiments on 10 LLMs, where Claude 3.5 Sonnect, the best-performing model, still cannot perform well in repository-level debugging.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RepoDebug, a new multi-task and multi-language repository-level code debugging dataset.  RepoDebug is designed to address the limitations of existing code debugging datasets, which primarily focus on function-level repairs and lack diversity in tasks, languages, and error types. The dataset covers 8 programming languages, includes 3 debugging tasks (Bug Identification, Bug Localization, and Automatic Program Repair), and features 22 distinct error subtypes systematically classified into syntax, logic, reference, and multiple errors. The paper also presents evaluation experiments using 10 different Large Language Models (LLMs), demonstrating the challenges faced by even the best-performing models in repository-level debugging. The paper emphasizes the complexity of repository-level debugging, especially when the number of errors increases and code length grows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the construction of a repository-level debugging dataset that is both multi-task and multi-lingual.  While existing datasets like SWE-Bench operate at the repository level, RepoDebug distinguishes itself by offering a more structured and diverse range of error types, tasks, and languages, offering a broader scope for evaluating LLMs for code debugging. The structured taxonomy of 22 error subtypes is a valuable addition.

*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a critical gap in the evaluation of LLMs for code debugging – the ability to handle complex, realistic repository-level scenarios. By demonstrating the limitations of current LLMs on the RepoDebug dataset, the paper underscores the need for further research in this area.  The findings suggest that LLMs struggle with code semantics, multiple error types, and longer code contexts, highlighting areas where improvements are needed. It could potentially guide future research in prompt engineering, model architectures, and training data for code-related tasks.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dataset is well-motivated, addressing a clear need in the field.
    *   The creation process is rigorous, involving AST-based bug injection and manual validation.
    *   The dataset's diversity in languages, tasks, and error types makes it a valuable benchmark.
    *   The experimental results provide insights into the strengths and weaknesses of various LLMs for code debugging.
    *   The detailed analysis of error types, error counts, and code length further strengthens the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The dataset size, while substantial, could still be considered limited compared to some other code datasets.  Expanding the number of repositories and code files would increase the dataset's robustness.
    *   The paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of the types of errors that LLMs struggle with most at the repository level. While it broadly classifies error types, a more granular analysis of specific error subtypes would be more informative.
    *   The models in consideration are restricted to a certain context window; considering models capable of processing longer contexts would be pertinent in repository debugging.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Motivating researchers to focus on repository-level code debugging.
    *   Providing a valuable benchmark for evaluating and comparing different LLMs.
    *   Guiding the development of new techniques for improving LLMs' code debugging capabilities.
    *   Encouraging the creation of more realistic and diverse code debugging datasets.

*The code and dataset are released, promoting reproducibility and further research.*

Overall, this is a solid paper that makes a tangible contribution by providing a valuable new dataset and benchmark. The significance of addressing real-world LLM software debugging is critical in improving the usability of these models.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper's novelty, strong motivation, and potential influence justify a high score.  While there are areas for improvement, such as expanding the dataset size and conducting a more granular analysis of error subtypes, the paper addresses a significant gap in the field and provides a useful resource for future research. The dataset is well constructed, the experiments are well-conducted, and the results are clearly presented.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Intermediate Languages Matter: Formal Languages and LLMs affect Neurosymbolic Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04083v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Beiser, David Penz, Nysret Musliu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) achieve astonishing results on a wide range of tasks. However, their formal reasoning ability still lags behind. A promising approach is Neurosymbolic LLM reasoning. It works by using LLMs as translators from natural to formal languages and symbolic solvers for deriving correct results. Still, the contributing factors to the success of Neurosymbolic LLM reasoning remain unclear. This paper demonstrates that one previously overlooked factor is the choice of the formal language. We introduce the intermediate language challenge: selecting a suitable formal language for neurosymbolic reasoning. By comparing four formal languages across three datasets and seven LLMs, we show that the choice of formal language affects both syntactic and semantic reasoning capabilities. We also discuss the varying effects across different LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Intermediate Languages Matter: Formal Languages and LLMs affect Neurosymbolic Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of choosing different formal languages as intermediate representations in neurosymbolic reasoning systems that combine Large Language Models (LLMs) and symbolic solvers.  The core argument is that the choice of formal language significantly influences the performance of such systems, affecting both the syntactic correctness of the LLM's translation and the overall semantic reasoning accuracy. The authors conduct experiments using four formal languages (Pyke, ASP, NLTK, and FOL), three reasoning datasets (ProntoQA, ProofWriter, FOLIO), and seven LLMs (varying in size).  Their results indicate that First-Order Logic (FOL) generally outperforms logic programming languages in this neurosymbolic reasoning context, and the performance varies across different LLMs. The paper introduces the "intermediate language challenge" as a research problem for improving neurosymbolic reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of systematically evaluating the impact of different formal languages in neurosymbolic reasoning is somewhat novel. While previous works have employed intermediate languages, a focused comparative study is valuable. However, the novelty is somewhat limited because the datasets and LLMs are not new, and the concept of neurosymbolic reasoning is already established.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a practical contribution by highlighting the importance of a previously overlooked factor in neurosymbolic reasoning design. Understanding the strengths and weaknesses of various formal languages in conjunction with LLMs can guide the development of more effective reasoning systems. The paper also empirically demonstrates that one size does not fit all and some LLMs are better at some formal languages than others. This is significant because it is a step toward creating a taxonomy of LLMs and formal languages to facilitate building the most effective reasoning systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper's strength lies in its comprehensive empirical evaluation across multiple formal languages, datasets, and LLMs. This provides a solid foundation for the conclusions drawn.
    *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly defines the "intermediate language challenge" and frames the research around it.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The paper attempts to increase reproducibility by using existing datasets, and relatively well-known formal languages (though details of prompting and system implementation could be more extensive to further facilitate independent replication).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The choice of only four formal languages limits the scope of the investigation. It would be interesting to see how custom languages or other variations fare.
    *   **Lack of Deeper Explanation:** While the paper demonstrates the impact of formal languages, it doesn't fully explain *why* certain languages perform better. More analysis of the types of errors LLMs make with each language would be beneficial. Is it purely a syntactic issue, or are there semantic aspects at play as well?
    *   **Limited Baselines:** There are limitations with the baselines. The baselines may not represent state-of-the-art performance on those datasets.
    *   **Shallow treatment of reasoning model**: The authors are quick to state that there are no issues relating to computational tractability, but a shallow treatment is given to how the reasoning model is constructed.
    *   **Limited datasets**: The datasets are limited, especially with FOLIO requiring classical logic concepts making it effective for Pyke and ASP.
    *   **Limited formal languages:** The choice of formal languages is limited and it's not clear how or why those ones were selected.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper can influence future research in neurosymbolic reasoning by encouraging researchers to carefully consider and justify their choice of formal language. It could also inspire the development of adaptive systems that automatically select the best formal language for a given task or LLM. The paper could motivate more in-depth analysis of the interaction between LLMs and formal language syntax.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    The paper presents a worthwhile empirical study that highlights an important consideration in neurosymbolic reasoning. However, the limited scope (number of formal languages and datasets), the lack of a deeper theoretical explanation of the results, and limited baselines prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. Therefore, a score reflecting these factors is warranted.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Towards Stable and Personalised Profiles for Lexical Alignment in Spoken Human-Agent Dialogue
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04104v1)
- **Authors**: Keara Schaaij, Roel Boumans, Tibor Bosse, Iris Hendrickx
- **Abstract**: Lexical alignment, where speakers start to use similar words across conversation, is known to contribute to successful communication. However, its implementation in conversational agents remains underexplored, particularly considering the recent advancements in large language models (LLMs). As a first step towards enabling lexical alignment in human-agent dialogue, this study draws on strategies for personalising conversational agents and investigates the construction of stable, personalised lexical profiles as a basis for lexical alignment. Specifically, we varied the amounts of transcribed spoken data used for construction as well as the number of items included in the profiles per part-of-speech (POS) category and evaluated profile performance across time using recall, coverage, and cosine similarity metrics. It was shown that smaller and more compact profiles, created after 10 min of transcribed speech containing 5 items for adjectives, 5 items for conjunctions, and 10 items for adverbs, nouns, pronouns, and verbs each, offered the best balance in both performance and data efficiency. In conclusion, this study offers practical insights into constructing stable, personalised lexical profiles, taking into account minimal data requirements, serving as a foundational step toward lexical alignment strategies in conversational agents.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty, significance, and potential impact.

**Summary**

The paper "Towards Stable and Personalised Profiles for Lexical Alignment in Spoken Human-Agent Dialogue" investigates the construction of stable, personalized lexical profiles for conversational agents to facilitate lexical alignment with users, particularly in contexts where user input is limited or inconsistent. The authors explored the amount of transcribed spoken data and the number of items per part-of-speech (POS) category needed to create these profiles. Using data from interviews with Dutch veterans, they evaluated profile performance (recall, coverage, and cosine similarity) across time. The results suggest that smaller, more compact profiles constructed from 10 minutes of transcribed speech, with specific item numbers per POS category (5 for adjectives/conjunctions, 10 for others), offer a good balance between performance and data efficiency.
