### How many patients could we save with LLM priors?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04250v1)
- **Authors**: Shota Arai, David Selby, Andrew Vargo, Sebastian Vollmer
- **Abstract**: Imagine a world where clinical trials need far fewer patients to achieve the same statistical power, thanks to the knowledge encoded in large language models (LLMs). We present a novel framework for hierarchical Bayesian modeling of adverse events in multi-center clinical trials, leveraging LLM-informed prior distributions. Unlike data augmentation approaches that generate synthetic data points, our methodology directly obtains parametric priors from the model. Our approach systematically elicits informative priors for hyperparameters in hierarchical Bayesian models using a pre-trained LLM, enabling the incorporation of external clinical expertise directly into Bayesian safety modeling. Through comprehensive temperature sensitivity analysis and rigorous cross-validation on real-world clinical trial data, we demonstrate that LLM-derived priors consistently improve predictive performance compared to traditional meta-analytical approaches. This methodology paves the way for more efficient and expert-informed clinical trial design, enabling substantial reductions in the number of patients required to achieve robust safety assessment and with the potential to transform drug safety monitoring and regulatory decision making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for using Large Language Models (LLMs) to inform prior distributions in hierarchical Bayesian modeling of adverse events (AEs) in multi-center clinical trials. Instead of data augmentation, the method directly extracts parametric priors from LLMs.  The researchers systematically elicit informative priors for hyperparameters within a hierarchical Poisson-Gamma model using pre-trained LLMs (Llama 3.3 and MedGemma). They demonstrate, via temperature sensitivity analysis and cross-validation on a real-world non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC) dataset, that LLM-derived priors improve predictive performance compared to traditional meta-analytical priors. Furthermore, they show that the approach can reduce the number of patients needed in clinical trials to achieve comparable statistical power, indicating potential cost savings and reduced patient burden.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in the systematic application of LLM-derived priors in hierarchical Bayesian modeling specifically within the context of clinical trial adverse event modeling. While prior work exists on using LLMs for prior elicitation in other domains and on data augmentation in various contexts, this study focuses on a specific and important problem in clinical trial design. The direct extraction of parametric priors, compared to data augmentation, offers a more transparent and interpretable approach, which is crucial in clinical settings. The paper also contrasts the use of a general LLM (Llama 3.3) with a biomedical-specific one (MedGemma), providing insights into their comparative performance. The temperature sensitivity analysis and comparative analysis of different prompting strategies (Blind vs. Disease-Informed) add to the methodological novelty.

**Significance:** The potential impact of this research is significant. Clinical trials are expensive and time-consuming. Reducing the required sample size, while maintaining or improving statistical power, can lead to substantial cost savings and accelerate drug development. The ability to incorporate expert knowledge from LLMs directly into the modeling process is valuable, particularly when domain expertise is available but difficult to quantify. The focus on adverse event modeling is crucial for drug safety assessment and regulatory decision-making. The use of a publicly available dataset further enhances the significance by promoting reproducibility and facilitating comparison with future research.  The finding that LLM-derived priors improve predictive accuracy compared to established meta-analytical methods is practically important. The demonstration of sample efficiency, where LLM-informed priors allow for comparable performance with less data, highlights the potential of the framework.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** Addresses a well-defined and important problem in clinical trial design.
*   **Systematic Methodology:** Presents a clear and reproducible methodology for LLM-based prior elicitation, including temperature sensitivity analysis and prompting strategy evaluation.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:**  Employs cross-validation and sample efficiency experiments on a real-world clinical trial dataset.
*   **Comparison to Baseline:**  Compares LLM-derived priors to established meta-analytical priors, providing a strong benchmark.
*   **Demonstrated Sample Efficiency:**  Shows that LLM-informed priors enable substantial reductions in sample size.
*   **Focus on Transparency and Interpretability:** The extraction of parametric priors (compared to augmentation) provides greater transparency and interpretability, important for adoption in medical contexts.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:**  The evaluation is limited to a single disease area (NSCLC) and dataset.  Generalizability to other disease areas and types of clinical trials needs further investigation.
*   **Limited LLM Architectures:** The study uses only two LLM architectures (Llama 3.3 and MedGemma). It would be beneficial to evaluate a broader range of LLMs.
*   **Lack of Formal Prior Informativeness Assessment:** The paper demonstrates improved predictive accuracy, but a more formal assessment of prior informativeness, and how it relates to improved performance, would strengthen the analysis.  A discussion of calibration would also be beneficial.
*   **Disease-Informed Prompts Not Consistently Better:**  The observation that Disease-Informed prompts didn't consistently outperform Blind prompts raises questions about the value of disease-specific information in this context and warrants further exploration.
* **No Assessment of Calibration**: The paper does not include any assessment of calibration of posterior predictions which would be crucial to evaluate practical validity

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5**. The paper presents a novel and significant approach with clear practical implications for clinical trial design. The systematic methodology, rigorous evaluation, and demonstrated sample efficiency are strong points. However, the limited scope of evaluation (single disease, two LLMs), the lack of formal prior informativeness analysis, and the inconsistent benefit of disease-informed prompts prevent a higher score. A more in-depth analysis of calibration and assessment of the quality of prior elicitation would also be beneficial to support the findings. A strong contribution, but further validation and generalization are needed.

### RL's Razor: Why Online Reinforcement Learning Forgets Less
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04259v1)
- **Authors**: Idan Shenfeld, Jyothish Pari, Pulkit Agrawal
- **Abstract**: Comparison of fine-tuning models with reinforcement learning (RL) and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) reveals that, despite similar performance at a new task, RL preserves prior knowledge and capabilities significantly better. We find that the degree of forgetting is determined by the distributional shift, measured as the KL-divergence between the fine-tuned and base policy evaluated on the new task. Our analysis reveals that on-policy RL is implicitly biased towards KL-minimal solutions among the many that solve the new task, whereas SFT can converge to distributions arbitrarily far from the base model. We validate these findings through experiments with large language models and robotic foundation models and further provide theoretical justification for why on-policy RL updates lead to a smaller KL change. We term this principle $\textit{RL's Razor}$: among all ways to solve a new task, RL prefers those closest in KL to the original model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper, "RL's Razor: Why Online Reinforcement Learning Forgets Less," investigates the phenomenon of catastrophic forgetting in foundation models when adapting to new tasks via fine-tuning. It compares Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning (RL) and finds that RL consistently preserves prior knowledge better than SFT, even when both achieve similar performance on the new task.  The authors identify a crucial factor: the KL divergence between the fine-tuned policy and the original (base) policy *on the new task*. They empirically demonstrate and theoretically justify that RL is implicitly biased towards KL-minimal solutions due to its on-policy nature.  This bias, termed "RL's Razor," means that RL tends to find solutions that are closer to the original model's distribution, thus reducing forgetting. The paper validates these findings through experiments with language models and robotic foundation models, and further corroborates the claim using a specially constructed "oracle SFT" which explicitly minimizes KL divergence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in identifying the KL divergence on the *new task* as a strong predictor of catastrophic forgetting and in establishing the link between RL's on-policy nature and its implicit KL-minimization bias. While prior work has explored catastrophic forgetting and its mitigation, this paper provides a clear, predictive, and theoretically supported explanation for the observed difference between SFT and RL. The introduction of the "RL's Razor" concept is a useful framing of this bias. This departs from simply addressing the symptoms of forgetting. Other findings such as those presented by Lai et al. (2025) are contrasted, adding to the paper's novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have significant implications for continual learning and adaptation of foundation models. It suggests that algorithms should explicitly aim to minimize KL divergence from the base model to achieve effective adaptation without sacrificing prior knowledge. This opens avenues for developing new training methods that combine RL's knowledge preservation with the efficiency of SFT. Furthermore, the empirical forgetting law provides a measurable proxy that helps in mitigating or adapting in a continual learning setting.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides extensive empirical evidence across diverse tasks and models (language and robotics) to support its claims. This includes controlled experiments like the ParityMNIST setup, which allows for systematic ablations.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The theoretical analysis, while potentially simplified, provides a solid foundation for understanding why on-policy RL leads to KL-minimal solutions.
    *   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents its findings, analysis, and arguments. The figures effectively illustrate the key concepts and results.
    *   **Well Explained Results:** The paper clearly outlines that other potential predictors were not the key predictors of catastrophic forgetting.
    *   **Reproducibility**:  Although the scope of the experiments is large, they are reasonably reproducible within the community.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Generality of Theory:**  The theoretical analysis is based on simplified settings (e.g., binary reward functions, finite state spaces). While it captures the core intuition, the extent to which it generalizes to more complex, high-dimensional environments is not fully clear. The theory is also predicated on the model being correctly specified.

    *   **Scalability and Open Questions**:  The conclusions for large-scale experiments are not fully explored and the paper mentions toy models and frontier scale problems.

    *   **Oracle SFT Limitations:** The Oracle SFT distribution minimizes KL in a limited, analytical setting. This may not be a directly applicable approach to high-dimensional, complex models.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of future continual learning algorithms for foundation models. The emphasis on KL minimization as a guiding principle could lead to new regularization techniques or training strategies. The practical forgetting law is easy to understand and will provide an easily applied tool for continual learning.
    *   Additionally, the limitations of the conclusions for large-scale experimentation temper an even larger impact.

*   **Score:** 8

**Rationale:** The paper makes a novel and significant contribution by identifying the importance of KL divergence on the *new task* as a predictor of catastrophic forgetting and linking it to RL's on-policy nature. The theoretical support and diverse empirical validation strengthens the conclusions. The theoretical simplification and potential limitations with frontier scale experiments prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. The paper provides excellent guidance and direction to guide continual learning, making it an important contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### TauGenNet: Plasma-Driven Tau PET Image Synthesis via Text-Guided 3D Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04269v1)
- **Authors**: Yuxin Gong, Se-in Jang, Wei Shao, Yi Su, Kuang Gong
- **Abstract**: Accurate quantification of tau pathology via tau positron emission tomography (PET) scan is crucial for diagnosing and monitoring Alzheimer's disease (AD). However, the high cost and limited availability of tau PET restrict its widespread use. In contrast, structural magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) and plasma-based biomarkers provide non-invasive and widely available complementary information related to brain anatomy and disease progression. In this work, we propose a text-guided 3D diffusion model for 3D tau PET image synthesis, leveraging multimodal conditions from both structural MRI and plasma measurement. Specifically, the textual prompt is from the plasma p-tau217 measurement, which is a key indicator of AD progression, while MRI provides anatomical structure constraints. The proposed framework is trained and evaluated using clinical AV1451 tau PET data from the Alzheimer's Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI) database. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach can generate realistic, clinically meaningful 3D tau PET across a range of disease stages. The proposed framework can help perform tau PET data augmentation under different settings, provide a non-invasive, cost-effective alternative for visualizing tau pathology, and support the simulation of disease progression under varying plasma biomarker levels and cognitive conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TauGenNet, a novel framework for synthesizing 3D tau PET images using a text-guided diffusion model. The model is conditioned on structural MRI and plasma p-tau217 measurements, leveraging the anatomical information from MRI and the disease progression indicator from plasma measurements.  The authors train and evaluate their approach using the ADNI database, demonstrating the generation of realistic and clinically meaningful 3D tau PET images across various disease stages. The framework aims to augment limited tau PET data, provide a non-invasive alternative for visualizing tau pathology, and simulate disease progression under different biomarker and cognitive conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates some novelty in integrating plasma p-tau217 measures as a textual prompt into a 3D diffusion model for tau PET image synthesis. While diffusion models have been used for medical image synthesis before, the specific combination of p-tau217 and MRI with a text-guided approach is a contribution. The transition from the authors' prior 2D work to 3D is also a step forward, as the inherently volumetric nature of PET scans necessitates a 3D approach.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the limited availability and high cost of tau PET imaging. Synthesizing realistic tau PET images can facilitate data augmentation, enable in-silico studies of disease progression, and support the development of machine learning models. By incorporating readily available plasma biomarkers, the approach offers a less invasive and more accessible method for understanding tau pathology.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Multi-Modal Fusion:** Effectively combines MRI and plasma p-tau217 data, leveraging the strengths of each modality.
    * **3D Image Synthesis:** Generating 3D images is a significant improvement over previous 2D approaches, capturing the spatial complexity of tau deposition.
    * **Clinical Relevance:**  The paper demonstrates the generation of clinically meaningful tau PET images across different disease stages, aligning with known AD pathology.
    * **Quantitative Evaluation:**  The authors employ a region-wise MSE analysis to quantitatively assess the quality of the generated images, stratified by plasma intervals.
    * **Clear and Well-Written:**  The paper is well-structured and clearly explains the methodology and results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Dataset:** The study utilizes a relatively small dataset (360 paired samples), which may limit the generalizability and robustness of the model.
    * **Variance Discrepancy:**  The generated images exhibit lower variance than real measurements, potentially due to the model's limitations in capturing the full complexity of the data.  The authors acknowledge this and attribute it to a lack of demographic and clinical variables in the model, which could be expanded upon.
    * **Over-Simplification:** The use of a simple text prompt for the plasma values may oversimplify the complex relationship between plasma biomarkers and tau deposition.  More sophisticated encoding methods might be beneficial.
    * **Diagnostic Utility:**  The authors acknowledge that the generated images are not yet suitable for clinical diagnosis.  The relatively high dynamic range of tau PET increases the risk of prediction error. Further model refinement is necessary.
    * **Lack of Ablation Studies:**  The paper lacks thorough ablation studies to demonstrate the specific contribution of different components, such as the cross-attention mechanism or the MRI conditioning.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper provides a promising avenue for addressing the data scarcity challenge in tau PET imaging. If further developed, it could facilitate more accessible and cost-effective research on Alzheimer's disease. The framework's ability to simulate disease progression has potential for developing and evaluating therapeutic interventions.

* **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a novel approach with significant potential, but is currently limited by the small dataset, variance discrepancies, and over-simplification in its design. The demonstration of clinical relevance is encouraging, yet the current model is not suitable for direct diagnostic applications. Given these factors, the novelty and limitations place it at a moderate level in terms of overall contribution.

### Inverse IFEval: Can LLMs Unlearn Stubborn Training Conventions to Follow Real Instructions?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04292v1)
- **Authors**: Qinyan Zhang, Xinping Lei, Ruijie Miao, Yu Fu, Haojie Fan, Le Chang, Jiafan Hou, Dingling Zhang, Zhongfei Hou, Ziqiang Yang, Changxin Pu, Fei Hu, Jingkai Liu, Mengyun Liu, Yang Liu, Xiang Gao, Jiaheng Liu, Tong Yang, Zaiyuan Wang, Ge Zhang, Wenhao Huang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve strong performance on diverse tasks but often exhibit cognitive inertia, struggling to follow instructions that conflict with the standardized patterns learned during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). To evaluate this limitation, we propose Inverse IFEval, a benchmark that measures models Counter-intuitive Abilitytheir capacity to override training-induced biases and comply with adversarial instructions. Inverse IFEval introduces eight types of such challenges, including Question Correction, Intentional Textual Flaws, Code without Comments, and Counterfactual Answering. Using a human-in-the-loop pipeline, we construct a dataset of 1012 high-quality Chinese and English questions across 23 domains, evaluated under an optimized LLM-as-a-Judge framework. Experiments on existing leading LLMs demonstrate the necessity of our proposed Inverse IFEval benchmark. Our findings emphasize that future alignment efforts should not only pursue fluency and factual correctness but also account for adaptability under unconventional contexts. We hope that Inverse IFEval serves as both a diagnostic tool and a foundation for developing methods that mitigate cognitive inertia, reduce overfitting to narrow patterns, and ultimately enhance the instruction-following reliability of LLMs in diverse and unpredictable real-world scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the "Inverse IFEval: Can LLMs Unlearn Stubborn Training Conventions to Follow Real Instructions?" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Inverse IFEval, a benchmark designed to evaluate the counterintuitive abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). It addresses the problem of LLMs exhibiting "cognitive inertia," struggling to follow instructions that contradict the standardized patterns they learned during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Inverse IFEval comprises eight challenge types that invert common training paradigms to assess a model's ability to override ingrained biases. The authors constructed a dataset of high-quality Chinese and English questions across various domains using a human-in-the-loop pipeline and evaluated leading LLMs.  Results highlight the necessity of adaptability under unconventional contexts in addition to fluency and factual correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The central idea of systematically evaluating *counterintuitive abilities* in LLMs is relatively novel. While adversarial attacks and robustness evaluations exist, Inverse IFEval focuses specifically on inverting training conventions to expose limitations in instruction following. This provides a new perspective on LLM evaluation that is complementary to existing benchmarks.
*   **Significance:** The paper highlights a crucial limitation in current LLMs: overfitting to training paradigms and the inability to adapt to atypical instructions. This has implications for real-world deployment, where user needs and instructions may deviate from idealized training scenarios. The benchmark can serve as a valuable tool for diagnosing and improving LLM robustness.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined problem:** Clearly articulates the problem of cognitive inertia and its impact on LLM performance.
    *   **Systematic approach:** Develops a structured benchmark with distinct challenge types targeting specific training conventions.
    *   **Comprehensive dataset:** Constructs a high-quality dataset with a rigorous human-in-the-loop pipeline.
    *   **Thorough experimentation:** Evaluates a range of leading LLMs and provides insightful analyses of their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Publicly available dataset:** Facilitates further research and development in this area.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Artificiality:** The deliberately constructed counterintuitive instructions, while exposing vulnerabilities, might not perfectly reflect the nuances of real-world user requests. While the authors acknowledge this, the degree to which this limits the findings' generalizability is worth considering.
    *   **Subjectivity:** The degree to which some instructions are "counterintuitive" can be debated and context-dependent.
    *   **Limited scope of solutions:** While the paper highlights the problem, it doesn't propose any specific architectural or training modifications to address the identified limitations. This would be a natural next step for future research.
    *   **Evaluation bias:** The judge LLM itself might struggle with inverse instructions, potentially skewing results. The reported 98% accuracy mitigates this concern, but the possibility exists.

*   **Potential Influence:** Inverse IFEval has the potential to influence the direction of LLM research by shifting the focus beyond fluency and factual accuracy to include adaptability and the ability to override ingrained biases. It could also stimulate the development of new training techniques and architectural designs that promote more robust instruction following.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by identifying a key limitation of current LLMs and providing a benchmark to evaluate it systematically.  It's well-executed and demonstrates that LLMs are often not as robust in following atypical directions as we might expect. While the paper's conclusions are compelling, the artificiality of the test cases and the absence of concrete solutions to the identified problem reduce its immediate impact.

Score: 7/10

### Facts Fade Fast: Evaluating Memorization of Outdated Medical Knowledge in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04304v1)
- **Authors**: Juraj Vladika, Mahdi Dhaini, Florian Matthes
- **Abstract**: The growing capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) show significant potential to enhance healthcare by assisting medical researchers and physicians. However, their reliance on static training data is a major risk when medical recommendations evolve with new research and developments. When LLMs memorize outdated medical knowledge, they can provide harmful advice or fail at clinical reasoning tasks. To investigate this problem, we introduce two novel question-answering (QA) datasets derived from systematic reviews: MedRevQA (16,501 QA pairs covering general biomedical knowledge) and MedChangeQA (a subset of 512 QA pairs where medical consensus has changed over time). Our evaluation of eight prominent LLMs on the datasets reveals consistent reliance on outdated knowledge across all models. We additionally analyze the influence of obsolete pre-training data and training strategies to explain this phenomenon and propose future directions for mitigation, laying the groundwork for developing more current and reliable medical AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Facts Fade Fast: Evaluating Memorization of Outdated Medical Knowledge in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a critical safety concern in applying Large Language Models (LLMs) to healthcare: the memorization and subsequent reliance on outdated medical knowledge.  Medical understanding evolves rapidly, and LLMs, trained on static datasets, risk providing harmful or incorrect advice. To investigate this, the authors introduce two novel question-answering (QA) datasets: MedRevQA (general biomedical knowledge) and MedChangeQA (a subset where medical consensus has changed over time). They evaluate eight prominent LLMs and demonstrate consistent reliance on outdated knowledge across all models.  The paper also analyzes the influence of obsolete pre-training data and training strategies and proposes future directions for mitigation.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of the MedChangeQA dataset and its specific focus on *temporal decay* of medical knowledge within LLMs. While previous works have explored knowledge updating and temporal QA, they often focus on encyclopedic or general knowledge. The emphasis on medical knowledge, where outdated information can have serious consequences, is a significant contribution. Creating the MedChangeQA dataset from systematic reviews is a methodologically sound approach to capturing changing consensus. The MedRevQA dataset, while larger, is somewhat less novel, serving primarily as a broader benchmark.

**Significance:** The work is highly significant because it directly addresses a major barrier to the safe and responsible deployment of LLMs in healthcare. Demonstrating the propensity of state-of-the-art LLMs to rely on outdated medical facts is a crucial finding with implications for how these models are developed, trained, and deployed. The analysis of potential causes, like the prevalence of older studies in pre-training data, provides valuable insights for mitigation strategies. Highlighting the challenge of knowledge conflict resolution and the need for advanced RAG techniques further enhances the paper's impact.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined problem:** Clearly articulates the risk of using outdated medical knowledge in LLMs.
*   **High-quality datasets:** The creation of MedRevQA and MedChangeQA are significant contributions.  The use of systematic reviews ensures that the datasets are based on rigorous methodology and evidence.
*   **Comprehensive evaluation:** Tests a range of LLMs, including both open-source and proprietary models.
*   **Insightful analysis:** Explores potential causes for the observed reliance on outdated knowledge.
*   **Actionable suggestions:** Provides concrete directions for future research, including advanced RAG techniques and continual learning.
*   **Good writing and clear presentation of results:** The methodology, findings, and analysis are well-explained and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited scale of MedChangeQA:** While meticulously constructed, the dataset of only 512 change pairs is relatively small. This might limit the generalizability of the findings to all medical topics and types of knowledge change. A larger change set would strengthen the claims.
*   **RAG experiment is preliminary:** While a simple RAG setup improved scores, the implementation is basic. A more comprehensive investigation of advanced RAG techniques would have added more value to the paper.
*   **Lack of human medical expert involvement:** Despite the creation of the dataset focusing on medical knowledge, it lacks external validation of dataset labels.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development and deployment of medical AI systems in the following ways:

*   **Increased awareness:** Raises awareness among researchers and developers about the risks of outdated knowledge.
*   **Benchmarking:** Provides valuable benchmarks for evaluating the temporal awareness of LLMs.
*   **Research direction:** Inspires further research on mitigation strategies, such as advanced RAG and continual learning.
*   **Ethical considerations:** Highlights the ethical responsibility of ensuring that medical AI systems are up-to-date and reliable.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5** to this paper. While the paper addresses a critical problem and makes valuable contributions through the creation of datasets and insightful analysis, the limited scale of the MedChangeQA dataset and the preliminary nature of the RAG experiments hold it back from achieving a higher score. The novelty of the contribution lies more in the application of temporal QA to the high-stakes domain of medicine, rather than fundamentally new methodological developments. However, its potential influence on the field of medical AI makes it a valuable and impactful contribution. The lack of expert input lowers the score a bit, since this could ensure a higher quality and more nuanced dataset.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### Learning Optimal Crew Dispatch for Grid Restoration Following an Earthquake
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04308v1)
- **Authors**: Farshad Amani, Faezeh Ardali, Amin Kargarian
- **Abstract**: Post-disaster crew dispatch is a critical but computationally intensive task. Traditional mixed-integer linear programming methods often require minutes to several hours to compute solutions, leading to delays that hinder timely decision-making in highly dynamic restoration environments. To address this challenge, we propose a novel learning-based framework that integrates transformer architectures with deep reinforcement learning (DRL) to deliver near real-time decision support without compromising solution quality. Crew dispatch is formulated as a sequential decision-making problem under uncertainty, where transformers capture high-dimensional system states and temporal dependencies, while DRL enables adaptive and scalable decision-making. Earthquake-induced distribution network damage is first characterized using established seismic standards, followed by a scenario generation and reduction pipeline that aggregates probable outcomes into a single geospatial impact map. Conditioned on this map, the proposed framework generates second-level dispatch strategies, trained offline on simulated and historical events and deployed online for rapid response. In addition to substantial runtime improvements, the proposed method enhances system resilience by enabling faster and more effective recovery and restoration. Case studies, particularly on the 2869-bus European gas and power network, demonstrate that the method substantially accelerates restoration while maintaining high-quality solutions, underscoring its potential for practical deployment in large-scale disaster response.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical problem of post-earthquake grid restoration, focusing on optimal crew dispatch. It argues that traditional mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) approaches are too slow for real-time decision-making in rapidly evolving disaster scenarios. The authors propose a novel learning-based framework that integrates transformer architectures with deep reinforcement learning (DRL). This framework aims to provide near real-time decision support without sacrificing solution quality. The approach involves: (1) characterizing earthquake-induced distribution network damage using seismic standards, (2) generating a geospatial impact map via scenario generation and reduction, and (3) training a DRL-based dispatcher offline on simulated and historical events, and deploying this model online for rapid response. The effectiveness of the framework is demonstrated through case studies, particularly on the 2869-bus European gas and power network, highlighting substantial runtime improvements while maintaining high-quality solutions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Relevant Problem:** Post-disaster grid restoration is a crucial challenge, and the paper correctly identifies the limitations of traditional optimization methods in dynamic environments.
    *   **Novel Approach:** Integrating transformer architectures with DRL for crew dispatch is a significant departure from existing methods. The use of transformers allows for capturing complex system states and temporal dependencies, which is an improvement over memory-less policies.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper emphasizes practical deployment and real-time decision support, making it relevant to utility operators and disaster response agencies. The end-to-end system is designed to be usable after the earthquake.
    *   **Comprehensive Methodology:** The paper provides a detailed methodology, including earthquake modeling, scenario generation, power flow analysis, and reinforcement learning training, making it replicable.
    *   **Strong Numerical Results:** The case studies demonstrate substantial runtime improvements compared to traditional MILP-based approaches, using commercial solvers like CPLEX and Gurobi.
    *   **Scalability:**  Demonstration on the 2869-bus system shows potential for real-world application.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplified Seismic Hazard Modeling:** The assumption of known earthquake parameters (epicenter, magnitude, fault type) simplifies the seismic hazard modeling. While this focuses the work, it limits the consideration of more complex and realistic uncertainties. The paper mentions that uncertainty is captured via different runs. However, the lack of probabilistic methods may be a weakness.
    *   **Limited Comparative Baselines:** While compared against commercial solvers and a GA-based method, comparisons to other learning-based or more advanced heuristic techniques for crew dispatch could strengthen the contribution.
    *   **Dependency on High-Quality Data:** The performance of DRL models heavily relies on the quality and diversity of training data. The paper doesn't fully discuss the challenges of obtaining sufficient and representative training data for earthquake scenarios. It's also unclear whether the data is representative of any region.

    *   **Lack of real-world validation:** While the results show strong runtime and solution quality improvements in simulations, a critical weakness is the absence of real-world validation. The paper would greatly benefit from some assessment of performance with utilities.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The integration of transformers with DRL is novel in the context of power system restoration.

    *   The approach offers a potentially significant advancement in terms of speed and scalability for crew dispatch problems.
    *   The framework could enable more effective disaster response and enhance system resilience.
    *   The work lays a foundation for future research in learning-based decision-making for power system operations under uncertainty.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the identified strengths and weaknesses, and considering the novelty and significance, I would assign this paper a score of **7**. While the paper presents a novel and promising approach with solid numerical results, there are limitations in seismic hazard modeling, real-world validation, and comparison to baselines. However, the research addresses a critical problem with a novel approach and provides a foundation for future work in learning-based decision-making.

### EvoEmo: Towards Evolved Emotional Policies for LLM Agents in Multi-Turn Negotiation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04310v1)
- **Authors**: Yunbo Long, Liming Xu, Lukas Beckenbauer, Yuhan Liu, Alexandra Brintrup
- **Abstract**: Recent research on Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs) has demonstrated that agents can engage in \textit{complex}, \textit{multi-turn} negotiations, opening new avenues for agentic AI. However, existing LLM agents largely overlook the functional role of emotions in such negotiations, instead generating passive, preference-driven emotional responses that make them vulnerable to manipulation and strategic exploitation by adversarial counterparts. To address this gap, we present EvoEmo, an evolutionary reinforcement learning framework that optimizes dynamic emotional expression in negotiations. EvoEmo models emotional state transitions as a Markov Decision Process and employs population-based genetic optimization to evolve high-reward emotion policies across diverse negotiation scenarios. We further propose an evaluation framework with two baselines -- vanilla strategies and fixed-emotion strategies -- for benchmarking emotion-aware negotiation. Extensive experiments and ablation studies show that EvoEmo consistently outperforms both baselines, achieving higher success rates, higher efficiency, and increased buyer savings. This findings highlight the importance of adaptive emotional expression in enabling more effective LLM agents for multi-turn negotiation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "EvoEmo: Towards Evolved Emotional Policies for LLM Agents in Multi-Turn Negotiation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EvoEmo, an evolutionary reinforcement learning framework designed to optimize emotional policies for LLM agents engaged in multi-turn negotiations. It addresses the limitation of current LLM agents which mostly generate passive, preference-driven emotional responses in negotiations. EvoEmo models emotional state transitions as a Markov Decision Process and uses population-based genetic optimization to evolve high-reward emotion policies across different negotiation scenarios. The framework includes an evaluation setup comprising vanilla and fixed-emotion strategies as baselines. Experimental results show that EvoEmo consistently outperforms these baselines, achieving higher success rates, higher efficiency, and increased buyer savings. The paper highlights the importance of adaptive emotional expression in enabling more effective LLM agents for negotiation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the **EvoEmo framework itself**. It is a unique combination of evolutionary reinforcement learning specifically tailored for optimizing emotional policies in LLM-driven negotiation agents. The idea of using genetic algorithms to evolve emotion transition rules is interesting. While RL and evolutionary methods are independently well-established, the application of their synergy to emotion modeling within LLM agents is relatively new.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its focus on **enhancing LLM agent negotiation capabilities by incorporating strategic emotional expression**. This is a departure from the prevalent focus on purely rational or reactive emotional responses. If the claims hold, this could significantly improve the realism and effectiveness of LLM agents in interactive environments, especially those involving dynamic bargaining. Furthermore, it tackles the challenging area of sparse reward and high volatility inherent in negotiation settings, where traditional RL algorithms often struggle. The new benchmark provided by the EvoEmo framework will be helpful for advancing research in emotion-aware negotiation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current LLM agents in negotiations regarding tactical inflexibility, adversarial naivety, and strategic myopia.
*   **Well-Defined Framework:** EvoEmo is a well-defined and theoretically grounded framework with clear components like emotion-aware MDPs and the reward function.
*   **Comprehensive Experimental Setup:** The paper presents a well-designed experimental setup with comparative baselines, various LLMs, and performance metrics.  The ablation studies provides insight on the key design choices.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of EvoEmo over the baselines in terms of success rate, efficiency, and buyer savings.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Emotional Spectrum:** The study only considers seven basic emotions, which may oversimplify the complexity of human emotional expression.  More complex emotions and emotional blends could lead to even more effective negotiation strategies.
*   **Scenario Dependence:** The evaluation is based on a limited set of Craigslist bargain scenarios, raising concerns about the generalizability of the results to other negotiation domains (e.g., high-stakes situations or crisis negotiations).
*   **Interpretability Issues:** The evolutionary optimization process makes it difficult to interpret and understand the specific emotional sequences that contribute to successful negotiation outcomes.  It's a "black box" approach, making it hard to derive actionable insights about human negotiation behavior.
*   **Simulation-to-Reality Gap:** LLM simulations may not fully capture human behavior, which limits the real-world applicability of the developed strategies. The complexity and subtleties of human interaction cannot be fully captured in the simulations.
*   **Unethical consideration:** The paper does not discuss any potential risks involved in using emotional manipulation in artificial agents.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, the core idea of EvoEmo and its empirical validation are significant contributions. The framework addresses a clear gap in the existing literature and demonstrates a promising approach to improve LLM agents' negotiation capabilities by incorporating adaptive emotional strategies. The research methodology is well-structured, and the experimental results provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of EvoEmo. However, given the limitations regarding the emotional spectrum, scenario dependence, interpretability and the Simulation-to-Reality Gap, the significance is constrained.

### Write on Paper, Wrong in Practice: Why LLMs Still Struggle with Writing Clinical Notes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04340v1)
- **Authors**: Kristina L. Kupferschmidt, Kieran O'Doherty, Joshua A. Skorburg
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are often proposed as tools to streamline clinical documentation, a task viewed as both high-volume and low-risk. However, even seemingly straightforward applications of LLMs raise complex sociotechnical considerations to translate into practice. This case study, conducted at KidsAbility, a pediatric rehabilitation facility in Ontario, Canada examined the use of LLMs to support occupational therapists in reducing documentation burden.We conducted a qualitative study involving 20 clinicians who participated in pilot programs using two AI technologies: a general-purpose proprietary LLM and a bespoke model fine-tuned on proprietary historical documentation. Our findings reveal that documentation challenges are sociotechnical in nature, shaped by clinical workflows, organizational policies, and system constraints. Four key themes emerged: (1) the heterogeneity of workflows, (2) the documentation burden is systemic and not directly linked to the creation of any single type of documentation, (3) the need for flexible tools and clinician autonomy, and (4) effective implementation requires mutual learning between clinicians and AI systems. While LLMs show promise in easing documentation tasks, their success will depend on flexible, adaptive integration that supports clinician autonomy. Beyond technical performance, sustained adoption will require training programs and implementation strategies that reflect the complexity of clinical environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the real-world usability of Large Language Models (LLMs) in assisting occupational therapists with clinical documentation, specifically SOAP note generation at a pediatric rehabilitation facility.  It presents a qualitative case study based on interviews with 20 clinicians who participated in pilot programs using both a general-purpose proprietary LLM and a bespoke model fine-tuned on the organization's historical data.  The study reveals that despite technical capabilities, LLMs face significant sociotechnical barriers to effective implementation. These barriers stem from heterogeneous workflows, systemic documentation burdens, the need for clinician autonomy, and the importance of mutual learning between clinicians and AI systems.  The paper argues that successful LLM integration requires flexible, adaptive approaches that support clinician autonomy and reflect the complexity of clinical environments, emphasizing that simply automating tasks is insufficient.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Real-world Context:** The paper's strength lies in its grounding within a practical clinical setting. It moves beyond theoretical possibilities to examine the actual challenges of implementing LLMs in a real-world environment.
    *   **Qualitative Depth:** The qualitative approach, using interviews, provides rich, nuanced data, revealing the intricate sociotechnical factors that influence adoption. The direct quotes from clinicians add credibility and depth.
    *   **Focus on Sociotechnical Issues:** The paper effectively highlights that technological capability alone does not guarantee successful implementation. The emphasis on workflows, organizational policies, and clinician autonomy is crucial.
    *   **FITT Framework Application:** The application of the FITT (Fit between Individuals, Task, and Technology) framework is appropriate and provides a structured lens through which to analyze the results.
    *   **Critical Perspective:** The paper critically examines the "low-hanging fruit" assumption often associated with clinical documentation, offering a much more nuanced and cautionary perspective.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The case study approach, while providing depth, limits the generalizability of the findings to other settings or healthcare domains. Though it argues the lessons are broadly applicable, more evidence would strengthen this claim.
    *   **Model Specifics:** The paper describes the general model being used, but doesn't provide a lot of specifics regarding the model size, training data, or model fine-tuning other than it was "based on the Llama 3 8B architecture." Providing more of these technical details would enhance the reproducibility of this work.
    *   **Lack of a Clear Solution:** While the paper effectively identifies the problems, it offers less in the way of concrete solutions or design recommendations beyond general principles (e.g., "building adaptable tools"). More specific suggestions for tool design and implementation strategies would be valuable.
    *   **Pilot Study Details:** The information about the pilot studies is somewhat limited. More details on the pilot studies, particularly their design and duration, would provide a more robust context for the interview data.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper's novelty lies in its detailed qualitative exploration of the sociotechnical barriers to LLM adoption in clinical documentation within a *specific* healthcare context (pediatric occupational therapy). While other studies have addressed similar issues, this paper provides a particularly rich and nuanced account. The findings challenge simplistic assumptions about the ease of automating clinical documentation and highlight the importance of context-aware implementation. This is especially relevant as healthcare systems rapidly adopt these tools, and need to consider implementation as more of an organizational learning curve.
    The significance of the paper is in its contribution to a more realistic and grounded understanding of AI implementation in healthcare. It provides valuable insights for researchers, developers, and healthcare organizations seeking to integrate LLMs effectively. It highlights that a sole focus on technological performance can lead to failures and emphasizes the need for a holistic approach that considers human factors, workflows, and organizational context.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a detailed, qualitative analysis of LLM implementation challenges in a specific clinical setting. It effectively critiques the simplistic view of clinical documentation as a straightforward task for automation and highlights the complex sociotechnical factors involved. However, its limitations in generalizability, specifics of the model itself, and a lack of concrete solutions prevent it from achieving a higher score.
